vpsfort

vpsfort

Behaviour-based intrusion prevention for a small self-hosted server family: an XMPP server (ejabberd) with Nextcloud, a WireGuard VPN host and a Telegram relay node. Everything is written in Rust and runs as single static binaries on Linux (musl; x86_64, aarch64 and 32-bit ARMv7 with hardware floating point).

vpsfort follows the systemd journal (ejabberd, sshd, the UFW or firewalld kernel log, Nextcloud AIO), scores every remote address by what it does, and bans attackers in nftables, firewalld, ufw, iptables or ipset within seconds. It can also drop whole countries or networks (ASNs), by the offline geo database it already uses for labels. Nodes can share bans and suspicion reports over an encrypted cluster link. Six optional bots carry vpsfort's admin alerts, daily digest and weekly abuse report to the admins, and an optional web console shows the whole cluster to its admins, read only. They only carry messages and show data: protection never depends on them.

        ┌──────────── server ─────────────┐        cluster (XChaCha20-Poly1305, TCP 7946)
journal ─► vpsfort ─► nftables/firewalld/ufw/iptables/ipset ◄─► vpsfort on other nodes
            │  outboxes (files)
            ├─► vpsfort-xmpp-bot ─► XMPP, OMEMO-encrypted (client mode: else OpenPGP files)
            ├─► vpsfort-tg-bot     ─► Telegram (pseudonymized, files OpenPGP-encrypted)
            ├─► vpsfort-mail-bot   ─► SMTP, PGP/MIME-encrypted
            ├─► vpsfort-signal-bot ─► Signal through signal-cli (else OpenPGP files)
            ├─► vpsfort-matrix-bot ─► Matrix, Olm/Megolm-encrypted (else OpenPGP files)
            └─► vpsfort-push-bot   ─► ntfy, Gotify or a webhook (sanitized, files OpenPGP-encrypted)
            │  read-only console socket
            └─► vpsfort-web (agent) ◄─ web key, TCP 7949 ─ vpsfort-web (console) ─► HTTPS ─► the admin's browser

How vpsfort compares with fail2ban, sshguard, CrowdSec, CSF, Wazuh and OSSEC, the host integrity and monitoring tools, and commercial products, with pros and cons per category and feature: COMPARISON.md.

Crates

Each directory is its own Cargo project with a README that covers setup, settings and design in detail.

cratewhat it does
vpsfortThe daemon and its CLI/TUI: log parsers, the scoring and ban engine, firewall backends, cluster, exposure/cron/antivirus watches, package checks (dpkg or rpm), blocklist feeds, country and network blocking, daily digest, weekly abuse reports.
vpsfort-xmpp-botXMPP bot in two modes. Admin (on the XMPP server): curated ejabberdctl commands in chat, plus vpsfort's admin messages over OMEMO. Client (BOT_MODE=client, any host and XMPP server, e.g. a desktop): send-only, vpsfort's admin messages over OMEMO, else as OpenPGP-encrypted files. SCRAM-SHA-256-PLUS login.
vpsfort-tg-botTelegram transport for vpsfort's admin messages. Account names become pseudonyms and servers appear only by node id. Reports go out as OpenPGP-encrypted files.
vpsfort-mail-botSend-only SMTP bot that mails vpsfort's admin messages, each OpenPGP-encrypted as a whole (subject included).
vpsfort-signal-botSend-only Signal bot over signal-cli (installed separately): vpsfort's admin messages while the safety number with the admin is the pinned one, else as OpenPGP-encrypted files.
vpsfort-webRead-only web console for the whole cluster: an agent on every node hands its vpsfort's data (through a read-only socket) to the console, sealed with a web key of its own (never the cluster key) and forward-secret; the console shows bans, alerts, offenders, places and every node's health and every node's abuse report split per network owner, ready to mail, over HTTPS (TLS 1.3) to its admins (one or a few, all equal), each with a password and a one-time code or a passkey, plus /metrics for Prometheus. It also runs alone on a host without vpsfort, as an aggregator.
vpsfort-simA simulated cluster and its web console in one process, from real decision logs (each node's state from vpsfort's own log reader, digest renderer and abuse report builder) or a console export, with real vpsfort-web agents and console: for trying the console without servers, screenshots and page work. Makes the web key, the admin's password and one-time secret and a certificate when missing, and says so.
vpsfort-matrix-botSend-only Matrix client: vpsfort's admin messages end-to-end encrypted (Olm/Megolm, vodozemac) to the devices the admin verified, else as OpenPGP-encrypted files.
vpsfort-push-botSend-only push bot for ntfy, Gotify or any webhook: vpsfort's admin messages sanitized as for Telegram (the service reads them), files only OpenPGP-encrypted.
vpsfort-botlibWhat the Telegram, mail, Signal, Matrix and push bots share: the outbox format, the pseudonyms and server names, the OpenPGP files, the log. A library, not shipped on its own.

The supported combinations of bots and cluster peers are described in vpsfort's README, "Deployment layouts". vpsfort/tests/layouts.rs runs each layout end to end.

Build and test

You need Rust stable and the x86_64-unknown-linux-musl target (for ARM also aarch64-unknown-linux-musl, armv7-unknown-linux-musleabihf; TARGET=<target> ./scripts/ci.sh builds one). The bots and vpsfort-web also need the llvm-tools component. Each crate builds and tests on its own:

cd vpsfort            # or vpsfort-xmpp-bot, vpsfort-tg-bot, vpsfort-mail-bot, vpsfort-signal-bot, vpsfort-matrix-bot, vpsfort-push-bot, vpsfort-web
./scripts/ci.sh        # fmt, clippy -D warnings, tests, cargo audit, cargo deny, static musl build
./scripts/bundle.sh    # the release bundle (zip); ./scripts/package.sh: the .deb, .rpm and .apk (both take --target TRIPLE)

package.sh needs cargo install cargo-deb cargo-generate-rpm --locked once; they write the packages without dpkg or rpm, so the packages build on macOS too.

The static Linux binary also builds from macOS without a C cross toolchain (see each crate's .cargo/config.toml). Some tests listen on 127.0.0.1, so they need a machine or sandbox that allows local sockets. .github/workflows/ runs the same ci.sh per crate on GitHub Actions and uploads the bundles: the tests on x86_64 and on GitHub's arm64 runners, the 32-bit ARM binary cross-built, linted for its target and started under qemu.

Each crate has fuzz/ targets for cargo-fuzz (nightly). tests/fuzz_smoke.rs runs the same targets on stable over their seeds.

Deployment

From a release bundle (no Rust needed): one zip per crate and architecture, <crate>-<version>-<commit>-<arch>.zip, <arch> one of x86_64-linux-musl, aarch64-linux-musl (arm64) and armv7-linux-musleabihf (32-bit ARMv7, hard-float). It holds the static binary, the systemd units, logrotate files and config templates, SHA256SUMS, and an install.sh. Unzip it on the server and run sudo ./install.sh (the first run of each puts a config in place to edit, then you run it again). Each crate's README has a section "Install from a release bundle", with how to download and verify one.

From a package (Debian, Ubuntu and their derivatives; Fedora, RHEL and its rebuilds; Alpine): the same releases carry <crate>_<version>-1_<arch>.deb (amd64, arm64, armhf), <crate>-<version>-1.<arch>.rpm (x86_64, aarch64, armv7hl) and <crate>-<version>-r0.<arch>.apk (x86_64, aarch64, armv7), the same static binary and deploy files for the package manager: sudo apt install ./<file>.deb, sudo dnf install ./<file>.rpm or apk add --allow-untrusted ./<file>.apk (the .apk is unsigned; its .sha256 and attestation verify it, and it brings OpenRC scripts instead of the units: Alpine has no systemd, and no journal, so vpsfort follows /var/log/messages there). vpsfort goes to /usr/bin with its units in /usr/lib/systemd/system and the templates in /usr/share/vpsfort/templates; the bots and vpsfort-web stay in /opt/<crate>. Nothing starts on its own: the first install puts the config template in place to edit, then you enable the service. An upgrade keeps the config (asking, when a terminal is there, whether to replace it with the new template) and restarts a running service when its config is valid. The packages also install over a bundle or deploy.sh install and take it over, keeping its config, keys and state. Rollback is installing the previous version's package. Each crate's README has a section "Install from a package".

From the repository (a public repository with GitHub Pages): .github/workflows/repository.yml runs after every release is published, gathers the newest .deb and .rpm of every crate from the releases, builds a signed apt repository (deb/, suite stable, component main) and a signed dnf repository per architecture (rpm/<arch>/) with scripts/repo.sh, and pushes them to the gh-pages branch, which Pages serves at https://makhort.github.io/vpsfort/ with the instructions on its index page; the rpms in it are signed with the same key (dnf checks each package, gpgcheck=1), the debs are covered by the signed InRelease. The same workflow builds the documentation site under docs/ there (the READMEs and COMPARISON.md with mdBook, scripts/docs-site.sh) after every push to main that changes them, also without a signing key. It needs the repository secret REPO_GPG_KEY (an armored OpenPGP private key made for this: gpg --quick-generate-key "vpsfort packages <you@example.org>" rsa4096 sign 0; RSA, since rpm before 4.16 (RHEL 8) can't verify ed25519 signatures; exported with --export-secret-keys), REPO_GPG_PASSPHRASE if it has one, and Pages set to deploy from the gh-pages branch (the workflow's first run creates it: choose it in Settings → Pages after that); without the key, or before the first release, only the documentation is published. Then:

sudo install -d -m 0755 /etc/apt/keyrings
curl -fsSL https://makhort.github.io/vpsfort/vpsfort.asc | sudo install -m 0644 /dev/stdin /etc/apt/keyrings/vpsfort.asc
echo "deb [signed-by=/etc/apt/keyrings/vpsfort.asc] https://makhort.github.io/vpsfort/deb stable main" | sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/vpsfort.list
sudo apt update && sudo apt install vpsfort

and on Fedora or RHEL sudo curl -fsSL https://makhort.github.io/vpsfort/vpsfort.repo -o /etc/yum.repos.d/vpsfort.repo && sudo dnf install vpsfort. Compare the key's fingerprint on the index page with the one you made. apt upgrade and dnf upgrade then bring new versions, with the same post-install steps as a single package.

The signing key's fingerprint is 12A75B42C4ADD340896F5E7A2F64B712E37F3CEC; compare it with the downloaded key's before the first apt update: gpg --show-keys /etc/apt/keyrings/vpsfort.asc.

Releases: a version is a tag <crate>-v<version> (vpsfort-v0.6.1, vpsfort-xmpp-bot-v0.6.1, ...), with the version in that crate's Cargo.toml and the release notes as that version's ## <version> section in the crate's CHANGELOG.md (./scripts/bundle.sh --check-tag TAG checks both). Pushing the tag runs that crate's workflow, which refuses a tag that doesn't match or has no notes, tests, builds and installs the bundles and packages as on every commit (the .deb on the runner, over the bundle's install; the .rpm in Fedora and AlmaLinux containers, the .apk in an Alpine container), then attaches them to the GitHub Release of the tag, each with its .sha256 and (in a public repository) a signed build provenance attestation, .sigstore.json, that gh attestation verify checks, and a software bill of materials, <crate>-<version>.cdx.json (CycloneDX, from cargo cyclonedx: every crate in the binary with its version, license and pkg:cargo identifier, with its .sha256 and covered by the attestation). Every CI run, tagged or not, also keeps its zip, .deb, .rpm, .apk and SBOM as workflow artifacts.

Dependencies: cargo audit (known vulnerabilities, unsound code, yanked versions) and cargo deny (deny.toml: permissive licenses only, crates from crates.io only, two versions of one crate reported) run in every crate's ci.sh, locally and on every CI run. vpsfort's workflow also enforces on the runner's own kernel with the real nft, as root (tests/real_nft.rs, and the installed daemon in enforce mode: a ban, the table deleted and rebuilt, an unban); the other enforcement tests use a stand-in nft.

Fedora and RHEL: vpsfort's package_manager = "dnf" (its -dnf config templates, the .rpm's default on a host with dnf) reads rpm's database and asks dnf for pending updates instead of dpkg and apt; with firewalld as the firewall. Tried on a Fedora 44 server, step by step in vpsfort/TESTING-fedora.md; the results are in vpsfort's README, "Verified on the servers".

From a checkout: each crate has a deploy/ directory: a deploy.sh that runs ci.sh, builds the binary and copies everything to a host over SSH, and an install.sh to run there as root. It also holds the systemd units, logrotate files and config templates. scripts/bundle.sh builds the release bundle locally. vpsfort ships config templates for four roles: vpsfort.toml (XMPP server), vpsfort-standalone.toml (a host with SSH and UFW only, e.g. a VPN server), vpsfort-tg.toml (Telegram relay node) and vpsfort-desktop.toml (a Linux desktop or laptop), each also as a -dnf variant for Fedora and RHEL.

The templates use placeholder names and addresses (example.org, documentation ranges). Replace them, and the allowlist entries, with your own before deploying. Start in mode = "shadow", review the decisions, then switch to "enforce".

SSH hardening: hardening/harden-vps.sh, run from your laptop, turns SSH on a VPS (Debian/Ubuntu or Fedora/RHEL-like) into the gateway layout vpsfort's SSH audit checks: one key-only user forced into su or sftp, keys in a root-owned file, su limited by pam_wheel. It takes over a server hardened partly by hand too (-g keeps its user, --su works without root login or sudo). It tests the new login before it commits and rolls back otherwise; hardening/harden-vps.sh --help lists the options.

vpsfort compared

How vpsfort compares with the open-source and commercial tools you might use instead: a briefing for teams thinking about adopting it.

The state is as of 2026-11-06: vpsfort 0.6.1, its web console vpsfort-web 0.5.1, the bots (vpsfort-xmpp-bot 0.6.1, vpsfort-tg-bot and vpsfort-mail-bot 0.5.1, vpsfort-signal-bot and vpsfort-matrix-bot 0.3.1, vpsfort-push-bot 0.2.1) and vpsfort-sim 0.2.0 (the commit named under Method and sources), compared with the releases and documentation of the other tools as of 2026-10-25. Feature claims about vpsfort come from its README and code. Claims about the other tools come from their documentation, release pages and advisories (see Sources). Products change, so check a tool's current documentation before you decide on one detail.

Legend for the tables: ✓ built in · ◐ partly, or by your own configuration or script · – not available.

1. vpsfort in one minute

vpsfort is one static Rust binary for a self-hosted Linux server. It does what usually takes four or five separate tools:

  • It bans attackers in the kernel firewall seconds after they show themselves.
  • It watches the host for the ways intruders stay in.
  • It notices when its own view goes dark: a log source that stops, a firewall that was flushed, a scan that was killed.
  • It tells the admins over end-to-end-encrypted channels (or sanitized, to a push service), and warns the users whose accounts are under attack.
  • It shows the whole cluster on one read-only HTTPS page, and to Prometheus (vpsfort-web, optional).

Several servers share what they see over an encrypted peer-to-peer link. There is no central server and no vendor in between: the web console only asks the nodes, with a key of its own that can't make a ban.

journal ─► vpsfort ─► nftables / firewalld / ufw / iptables / ipset  ◄── encrypted cluster link ──►  vpsfort on other nodes
             │
             ├─► vpsfort-xmpp-bot ─► XMPP, OMEMO-encrypted
             ├─► vpsfort-tg-bot     ─► Telegram, pseudonymized, files OpenPGP-encrypted
             ├─► vpsfort-mail-bot   ─► e-mail, PGP/MIME-encrypted
             ├─► vpsfort-signal-bot ─► Signal (signal-cli), else OpenPGP files
             ├─► vpsfort-matrix-bot ─► Matrix, Olm/Megolm-encrypted, else OpenPGP files
             ├─► vpsfort-push-bot   ─► ntfy, Gotify or a webhook, sanitized, files OpenPGP-encrypted
             └─► vpsfort-web (read-only socket) ─► the cluster on one HTTPS page, /metrics for Prometheus

What one install covers, and what you would otherwise run:

vpsfort partusually done with
scoring and banning, subnet bans, tripwires, honeypots, canary accounts; built-in sources for common web, database and mail serversfail2ban, sshguard, CrowdSec
country and network (ASN) blockingCSF CC_DENY, ipset scripts with a GeoIP list
ban sharing between your servers, blocklist feedsCrowdSec, CSF cluster, ipset scripts
a WAF's decisions (CrowdSec, or any app with a list) enforced at the firewallCrowdSec's firewall bouncer
cron and persistence watch, package and set-uid check (dpkg or rpm), process watchAIDE, debsums, rpm -Va, rkhunter, Wazuh FIM
listeners compared with firewall rules, dead rules, Docker bypassLynis, external port scans, by hand
patch status (apt or dnf), certificate checksapticron, dnf-automatic, needrestart, an uptime or certificate monitor
SSH hardening audit (sshd's effective settings, key file permissions, a su/sftp gateway's layout)Lynis, ssh-audit, Wazuh SCA
quiet log sources, OOM kills, disk, memory, failed unitsMonit, Netdata, Prometheus
daily digest, weekly abuse report; live abuse reports per network owner with the e-mail ready; an attacker's evidence as a link for its hosting providerLogwatch, AbuseIPDB scripts, by hand
a read-only web console for the cluster, a REST API, Prometheus metricsGrafana with exporters, the CrowdSec Console, Wazuh's dashboard

Key numbers (measured, from the README):

  • ~10 MB static binary with no runtime dependencies (the optional web console is another ~4 MB).
  • ~10 MB RAM for a real month of traffic (~50 MB with the offline geo database). The hard limit is 128 MB, and it held in a synthetic flood of 450,000 sources.
  • A real month of logs (~210,000 records) replays through the full engine in about one second: 185 bans of 93 targets. No address that ever logged in was banned.
  • Tripwire ports banned about 7,500 scanners on first contact in that month.

2. Who it is for, and who it is not for

A good fit:

  • One to about ten Linux servers you run yourself: Debian or Ubuntu, or Fedora and RHEL with dnf and firewalld; x86_64 (the ARM builds are untried on servers, see below); systemd.
  • SSH, XMPP (ejabberd), Nextcloud and WireGuard hosts, and the common web, database and mail servers through built-in patterns (Apache, nginx, HAProxy, Prosody, MySQL/MariaDB, PostgreSQL, MongoDB, Redis/Valkey, Postfix, Dovecot, ownCloud). Other services count through regex patterns of your own.
  • Admins who want alerts on their phone, encrypted, with nothing about their users or attackers sent to a vendor.
  • Small machines: it is built to stay within a 128 MB memory limit next to the services it protects.
  • Linux desktops and laptops, with a profile of their own (not yet run on one).

Not a good fit today:

  • Fleets of dozens of hosts or more that need fleet management, roles, several users and compliance reports. vpsfort's web console shows dozens of nodes to a few equal admins, read only by design, without fleet management or roles. Look at Wazuh, the CrowdSec Console or a commercial EDR.
  • Web-application-heavy hosts that need a WAF, captchas or CDN integration. vpsfort can enforce a WAF's decisions at the firewall ([waf], with CrowdSec's formats built in), but it inspects no HTTP request itself and answers none: run CrowdSec AppSec, ModSecurity or Coraza for the detection, and a CDN or an HTTP bouncer for captchas.
  • Other platforms: Windows, macOS, BSD, Linux without systemd. ARM bundles (arm64 and 32-bit ARMv7) are built and tested in CI but haven't run on an ARM server yet. Fedora has been tried on one test server; RHEL and its rebuilds not yet.
  • Organisations that need a vendor: a support SLA, certified compliance evidence, or a 24/7 security operations team.

3. The field

Ban managers and intrusion-prevention tools, compared in sections 4 to 12:

toolkindlicenselanguagemodelstate in Oct 2026
vpsfortban manager and host watchMITRustdecaying scores per address across all services, own nftables table (or firewalld, ipset, ufw, iptables), peer-to-peer cluster0.6.0, new in Oct 2026
fail2banban managerGPL-2.0Python"jails": a regex filter and a firewall action per service1.1.0 is what distributions ship
sshguardban managerBSD-styleCbuilt-in attack parser, a score per address2.5.1 (2025)
reactionban managerAGPL-3.0–streams, regex and actions; a smaller, newer fail2ban alternative2.x
CrowdSeccollaborative IPSMIT (engine); console is SaaSGoparsers and scenarios, a local API, separate "bouncers", a crowd-sourced blocklist1.7 released; docs on the 1.8 line
CSF / LFDfirewall front end and login-failure daemonGPL-3.0 since v15Perliptables rules, bans, port-scan, process and file checks; hosting-panel UIsConfigServer closed in Aug 2025; community forks (and, reportedly, cPanel's)
WazuhXDR / SIEM platform, a fork of OSSECGPL-2.0C, Python; OpenSearch-based indexeragents, a manager, an indexer and a dashboard; FIM, SCA, CVE detection, active response4.14.x; 5.0 in beta
OSSEChost intrusion detectionGPL-2.0Cagent and manager, log rules, FIM, rootcheck, active response4.x, maintained by Atomicorp

Host, posture and monitoring tools, compared in sections 7 to 9:

  • File integrity: AIDE, Samhain, debsums.
  • Rootkit scanners: rkhunter, chkrootkit.
  • Kernel-event monitoring: auditd, Falco, Tetragon.
  • Host state as SQL: osquery.
  • Hardening audit: Lynis.
  • Patching: unattended-upgrades, needrestart.
  • Health and uptime: Monit, Netdata, Prometheus with Alertmanager, Uptime Kuma, blackbox_exporter.
  • Log digests: Logwatch, logcheck.

Commercial offerings are compared in section 13.

4. Attack detection and banning

Bottom line: for the services it knows, vpsfort detects more, and is more careful about false positives, than the generic tools. For any other service, the others ship far larger rule libraries.

featurevpsfortfail2bansshguardCrowdSecWazuhCSF/LFD
built-in parsers◐ sshd, ejabberd, kernel log (UFW, firewalld, iptables), Nextcloud, su/sudo, PAM, audit log, and 24 common services (web servers and proxies, databases, mail, a code forge, a password manager, identity providers, Matrix, OpenVPN, FTP, a media server)✓ dozens of filters◐ SSH and common mail/FTP daemons✓ hub of parsers and scenarios✓ thousands of rules✓ hosting stack
patterns of your own✓✓– compiled in✓✓✓
one score per address across all services✓◐ per jail, plus recidive✓◐ per scenario◐ correlation rules◐ per trigger
low-and-slow probing over hours✓ 12 h score◐ long findtime◐◐◐◐
escalating ban times✓ 1 h to 30 d✓ bantime.increment✓◐ profile expressions✓ repeated_offenders✓ LF_PERMBLOCK
subnet bans✓ grow from /24 towards /16–◐ fixed prefix◐ range-scoped scenarios–◐ LF_NETBLOCK, fixed
port scans and tripwire ports✓◐ own filter–✓◐✓
honeypot listeners (handshake proves the source; what the scanner sent is evidence)✓–––––
canary account names✓◐ own filter–◐ own scenario◐ own rule–
per-account alerts (distributed guessing, login after failures, new network, new SSH key)✓––◐◐ success after failures◐ LF_DISTATTACK
a recent login protects an address✓ per service◐ ignorecommand script––––
dry-run mode✓ shadow mode◐ dummy action–✓ simulation✓ alert-only by default✓ TESTING
replay old logs through the same engine✓◐ fail2ban-regex tests filters–✓ replay mode◐ wazuh-logtest–
XMPP server attacks✓ deep◐ own filter–◐ own parser◐ own decoder–
featurepros (vpsfort)cons (vpsfort), and who does better
ParsersDeep parsers for what it knows. An ejabberd TLS failure is tied to its address through the Erlang PID. OpenSSH 10's sshd-session lines are handled. A line counts only from its real sender, by journald's own fields, so a local user can't log a fake sshd line to get someone banned. [sources] apps adds built-in patterns for Apache, nginx, HAProxy, Prosody, MySQL/MariaDB, PostgreSQL, MongoDB, Redis/Valkey, Nextcloud, ownCloud, Postfix, Dovecot and its own web console, each taking the address only from where the service writes it, so a client can't get another address banned through a user name or path.The built-in services are tested against documented and forged log lines, and Apache's against two honeypots' logs, but not yet run on a host with them. FTP and everything else need [[patterns]]. fail2ban, CrowdSec and Wazuh ship ready filters for hundreds of services.
Own patternsLinear-time regex, so no catastrophic backtracking on hostile lines. The sender is pinned by unit, or by identifier and uid. test-pattern and a TUI tab show the lines read, matched, and matched without an address.A pattern is a regex, a kind and a weight. There are no multi-line or stateful scenarios like CrowdSec's leaky buckets or Wazuh's composite rules.
ScoringTwo decaying scores (10 min and 12 h half-life) add up every signal from every service. A scanner that touches SSH, XMPP and closed ports a little each still reaches a ban. Weights are calibrated on a real month of logs.One model for everything: tuning means weights and thresholds, not per-service rules. At first it is less obvious than fail2ban's "N failures in T minutes".
Escalation1 h → 6 h → 1 d → 7 d → 30 d per repeat offence. Offences are shared, so a repeat attacker escalates on every node of the cluster.fail2ban, sshguard and CSF escalate too, and CSF offers permanent bans. Only the cluster-wide counter is new here.
Subnet bansStarts at /24 (IPv6 /64) and grows towards /16 (/48) only as nearby bans appear, at most 2 bits a step. It never covers a protected address. With the geo database, it never grows into another provider's network.The provider boundary needs the [geo] ASN database. A stricter policy, such as blocking a whole network or country now, needs feeds. CSF blocks countries out of the box.
Tripwires and honeypotsOne SYN to an unused telnet, RDP or database port bans. Kernel tripwires log these hits from vpsfort's own nftables rules, so UFW's log limit (about 3 lines a minute) doesn't hide scans. Honeypot ports complete the TCP handshake, so their evidence can't be forged, and keep the scanner's first bytes (a Telnet login, an HTTP request) for the abuse report.A blocked SYN can carry a forged source address. vpsfort limits the damage: such evidence never bans an address that logged in recently, replies always pass, and with honeypots set it is worth half a ban. A spoofer can still get outsiders banned for an hour.
Canary accountsNo accounts to create. On a key-only host, ssh = ["*"] makes every unknown user name a ban. A successful login to a canary is a critical alert.fail2ban, CrowdSec and Wazuh can do this with a filter of your own. vpsfort just has it built in.
Account protectionDistributed guessing against one account from several addresses; a login right after failures (possible compromise); a login from a new network or provider for that account; a login with a never-seen SSH key.Covers XMPP, SSH, Nextcloud, the built-in services and your patterns only. Wazuh covers far more authentication sources (PAM, Windows, cloud services).
False positivesShadow mode lifts its own "bans" when the address later logs in, and says so. An address that logged in recently isn't banned for that service's typos and reconnects. Private, CGNAT, loopback and link-local addresses are never banned.Login trust is a heuristic. An attacker behind the same carrier NAT as a user gets the same excuse for that service's noise; real failures still raise an alert.
Testingreplay runs journal exports through the whole pipeline without touching the firewall, and opens the result in the TUI.CrowdSec's replay mode and cscli explain are as capable, and better documented for writing new scenarios.

5. Firewall enforcement

Bottom line: vpsfort's enforcement takes the most care not to break the server itself (Docker, flushed rulesets, replies, oversized feeds). It covers the Linux firewalls (nftables, firewalld, ipset, ufw, iptables) but no BSD or Windows ones, and CrowdSec reaches the HTTP layer and CDNs.

featurevpsfortfail2bansshguardCrowdSec bouncerWazuhCSF/LFD
firewall backends◐ nftables, firewalld, ipset, ufw, iptables (Linux only)✓ iptables, nftables, firewalld, ufw, pf, ipfw, …✓ iptables, ipset, nftables, firewalld, pf, ipfw✓ nftables, iptables/ipset, pf, Windows◐ iptables, firewalld, pf, Windows◐ iptables
bans expire in the kernel, even while the daemon is down✓ nft and ipset timeouts◐ some ipset actions–✓––
Docker-published ports covered without extra setup✓◐ DOCKER-USER chain by config◐◐ DOCKER-USER or forward hook by config◐◐ DOCKER option
per-source connection rate limits in the kernel✓––––✓ CONNLIMIT, PORTFLOOD
public blocklists loaded into the firewall✓ with sanity limits––✓ console blocklists–✓ csf.blocklists
country blocking✓ offline, from DB-IP Lite or a commercial database read as it is, plus networks by ASN––◐ paid lists or own profiles–✓ CC_DENY
HTTP-level responses (403, captcha, CDN)–◐ Cloudflare action–✓ nginx, Traefik, HAProxy, Cloudflare, …––
a WAF's or IPS's decisions blocked at the firewall✓ [waf]: CrowdSec built in; JSON, CSV, regex, plain lists for others◐ an action script of your own–✓ what a bouncer does◐ active response scripts–
featurepros (vpsfort)cons (vpsfort), and who does better
Where bans sitIts own nftables table at prerouting, right after connection tracking. It runs before ufw's chains and before Docker's DNAT, so your own ALLOW rules can't shadow a ban, and Docker ports are covered without touching DOCKER-USER. It never edits your rules. On Fedora and RHEL it can keep everything in firewalld instead: its own ipsets, a drop zone and a forwarding policy, visible with firewall-cmd. The ipset backend is iptables with one set lookup however many bans.Linux only: no pf, ipfw or Windows. The ufw fallback loses Docker coverage, rate limits and feeds; the firewalld backend has no rate limits or feeds, and its entries end only while vpsfort runs. The firewalld, ipset and nftables backends ran on a Fedora 44 test server; the plain iptables backend has not yet run on a real host.
ExpiryBan timeouts live in the kernel: if vpsfort stops, bans still end on time. After a reboot it re-applies what is still active.CrowdSec's firewall bouncer works the same way.
Self-healingDebian's and Ubuntu's nftables.conf starts with flush ruleset, so a restart of nftables deletes every ban. vpsfort notices within 30 s, rebuilds every set and chain, re-adds the bans with their remaining time, and raises a high alert. The same after firewall-cmd --reload or a firewalld restart, and after its iptables chains are flushed (both seen on the Fedora server). A single change the firewall program refuses is kept and tried again every 30 s, with a high alert while it keeps failing.fail2ban restores its chains too, but only at its next ban action.
Replies to the server's own connectionsBans drop after connection tracking, so replies always pass: DNS, mail relay, update mirrors, federated XMPP servers. A forged packet that gets such an address banned can't cut the server off.The chains dropped a test client on the Fedora server, and on a production server both pings and a TCP connection to addresses banned there got their replies.
Rate limitsPer-source [[ratelimit]] entries in the kernel, from the first packet, Docker ports included. status shows their drop counters.nftables backend only (status says so under the others). CSF has richer flood controls (SYN floods, per-port and per-connection limits).
FeedsDownloads with curl (the binary has no TLS library) and keeps the last good copy. It drops bogons, prefixes wider than /12, and anything overlapping your allowlist or peers. A refresh that suddenly covers far more address space is refused.A few public lists (Spamhaus DROP and ASN-DROP, FireHOL); no curated commercial lists. nftables, ipset and firewalld backends (firewalld: 50,000 networks per list). CrowdSec's blocklists are larger and updated more often.
Countries and networks[geoblock] drops whole countries (or all but some) and networks by AS number, from the DB-IP Lite databases it already holds for labels: no download, no second database, about 1 MB while the list is computed. Protected addresses are cut out exactly. China is about 15,000 networks, everything but one country about 28,000. On the Fedora server two blocked countries timed out and a third connected.The free DB-IP Lite data misplaces some addresses and is refreshed monthly: coarse, not a curated list; a commercial database (DB-IP's IP to Country as it is) can replace it. ufw and plain iptables can't hold it; firewalld takes at most 50,000 networks. CSF's CC_DENY and CC_ALLOW have been doing this for years; vpsfort's [geoblock] ports limits a country block to some ports (SSH and XMPP closed, the web site open) the same way.
HTTP layer[waf] blocks what a WAF decides at the firewall, for every port and, as bans, on every cluster node: CrowdSec's local API, cscli output and blocklist mirror are read as they are, any other app through JSON, CSV, regex or plain-list formats. Its decisions get the protections of an operator ban (allowlists, peers, recent logins), are lifted when the WAF drops them, and large blocklists go into sets of their own.No WAF of its own, and no HTTP-level response: a banned visitor just times out. No captcha, no ban at the CDN or edge. CrowdSec's remediation components and WAF cover that layer. [waf] is tested against CrowdSec's documented formats, not yet run against a real CrowdSec.

6. Sharing and threat intelligence

Bottom line: vpsfort shares well among your own servers and keeps every observation private. Like every tool here but CrowdSec, it has no crowd-sourced intelligence; that is CrowdSec's main strength.

featurevpsfortfail2bansshguardCrowdSecWazuhCSF/LFD
bans shared between your own servers✓ peer to peer, encrypted– scripts–✓ through one central local API✓ through the manager✓ LF_CLUSTER
suspicion below the ban threshold adds up across servers✓–––✓ the manager sees all events–
works without a central server✓ mesh, chain or star––––✓
crowd-sourced global blocklist–◐ AbuseIPDB actions–✓–◐ third-party lists
nothing about attackers leaves your servers by default✓✓✓– signals go to CrowdSec unless turned off✓ self-hosted✓
reports to the attackers' abuse desks✓ weekly, with evidence and contacts◐ complain and AbuseIPDB actions––––
offline country and network labels✓––✓✓✓
featurepros (vpsfort)cons (vpsfort), and who does better
ClusterXChaCha20-Poly1305 under a key derived from a fresh X25519 exchange per connection (the shared key authenticates the exchange; a recording stays unreadable if the key leaks later), and only peers may connect. Facts are de-duplicated and forwarded up to 3 hops. Suspicion is shared too, so an address that probes every server a little still adds up to a ban. A shared ban carries up to three of its evidence lines, worded as the abuse reports word them, so a peer's log and the console say what the source did, not only its score. A limited peer (peer_accept) can send messages but never ban anything. Optional node identities: each node signs its own facts and files with an Ed25519 key of its own, pinned on the others, so a broken-into node (or a stolen cluster key) speaks only for itself, and a node is dropped by deleting its key, without a new cluster key. Key rotation is staged in three rounds, without a gap.Without node identities, every unlimited key holder is fully trusted: a broken-into node can ban on the others under any name, for at most max_remote_ban. With them, it can still ban for itself, and still read what the others share until the cluster key is rotated. Keys are pinned by hand in each node's config, with no PKI or enrollment. Node identities run on the authors' five servers, each pinning the other four's keys and requiring signatures; a fact refused for its signature has not been seen yet. It is sized for a handful of nodes, with no fleet management.
Threat intelligenceYour data stays yours: no vendor sees your attackers or your users. The static feeds (Spamhaus, FireHOL) need no account.No network effect, as with every tool here but CrowdSec, which blocks addresses that attacked thousands of other servers before they reach yours; vpsfort knows what your own nodes saw, its feeds, and through [waf] what a CrowdSec next to it decides.
Abuse reportsA weekly report grouped by abuse mailbox (looked up with RDAP), each attacker with UTC log lines and account names withheld. Reports from several nodes are merged, and the result reaches the admin encrypted. The web console shows the last day's or week's at any time, merged across the nodes, one entry per network owner, each copied or saved as the e-mail to its abuse desk, or opened in your mail program with the mailbox looked up (RDAP); a network is marked as mailed, and one banned again after that shows "seen again". An address's page can be handed to its hosting provider as a link that needs no login, cut to what the abuse report would show (account names withheld, nodes numbered), good for a day to a week.You send the mails yourself: vpsfort never mails third parties, by design. There is no AbuseIPDB integration.
GeoDB-IP Lite by default, or a commercial database read as it is (DB-IP's CSVs; any CSV with a header row; a city-level one adds cities and coordinates): about 1.2 million ranges in ~40 MB, looked up in memory, with no request per lookup. Used in alerts, to limit subnet growth, to learn each account's providers, and to block countries and networks.Cities, and map markers at the addresses' coordinates, only with a city-level database (DB-IP's city lite needs the unit's memory raised); the free data's accuracy: on the authors' servers, a VPN exit in Vienna (M247) shows as Czechia.

7. Host integrity and intrusion detection

Bottom line: vpsfort covers the practical "how would someone stay in?" checklist on Debian and Ubuntu with no extra tools. It is not a kernel-level runtime sensor and not a compliance-grade FIM.

featurevpsfortWazuhAIDErkhunter, debsumsFalco, Tetragonosquery
persistence points watched out of the box (cron, at, timers, system and user units, SSH keys and host keys, PAM, sudoers, ld.so.preload, apt and dnf hooks and sources, network hooks, shell start-up files, ...)✓ every minute◐ default directories, 12 h scans; real time by config◐ distribution config, typically daily◐ some startup files✓ rules, real time◐ tables, scheduled queries
changed lines shown in the alert✓✓ report_changes––––
package files told apart from foreign ones (dpkg or rpm origin)✓––✓ debsums–◐ deb_packages, rpm_packages
package files checked against dpkg's or rpm's hashes✓ weekly–◐ own baseline✓ debsums––
set-uid program inventory✓◐ rootcheck◐ baseline◐◐ rules✓ suid_bin
outbound connections and reverse shells◐ checked every 60 s, containers' too◐ command monitoring––✓ syscall events◐ snapshots
new kernel modules, containers (privileged, host network, risky mounts), WireGuard peers✓ every 60 s, after a learning period◐ rootcheck, rules–◐ rkhunter: modules✓ rules◐ kernel_modules, docker_containers tables
programs running from /tmp, from memory or from deleted files✓ checked every 60 s◐ rootcheck–◐✓◐ by query
failed su and sudo✓✓––––
PAM failures (console, polkit, Cockpit), account changes, auditd stopped✓✓––◐ rules◐ by query
a login with a never-seen SSH key✓–––––
malware signatures◐ ClamAV results; changed files scanned with ClamAV or YARA◐ ClamAV, YARA, VirusTotal integrations–◐ rootkit signatures––
real-time kernel events–◐ who-data through auditd––✓ eBPF◐ evented tables
events copied off the host as they happen◐ alerts through bots and peers; the decision log mirrored to a peer at every sync, and a daily hash of it kept by the peers✓◐ Samhain's server–◐ falcosidekick◐ fleet server
signs of journal tampering (lost position, sealed-journal check)✓–––––
featurepros (vpsfort)cons (vpsfort), and who does better
Persistence watchIt knows where intruders hide, including the scripts crontab lines run (also behind sudo -u, flock, timeout), at-jobs, user-level units, every user's shell start-up, the network stack's hooks, the resolver and the host keys, the package sources and the CA store. It keeps fingerprints only, never contents, so no password hashes in alerts or state. Alert severity knows whether dpkg or rpm installed the file unchanged. A file reached under two names (Fedora's merged /usr/local/sbin) is watched once. Installers announce their changes (expect-change) so upgrades don't alert. One local account can raise at most 5 alerts an hour, so it can't flood the channel. The kernel wakes it (inotify on the watched directories), so a change is scanned within seconds and one undone within the minute is still seen.It doesn't see which process made the write: Falco and Tetragon do. The list of places is fixed, plus your own [cron] files; AIDE and Wazuh FIM cover any directory tree.
Package and set-uid checkA debsums-like check (or rpm -Va-like, with each package's own digest algorithm) plus a set-uid inventory. It reads at most 20 MB/s and drops what it read from the page cache, so it stays within the 128 MB unit. Diverted files, rpm's %config files and upgrades in progress are handled. On the Fedora server it matched 7,472 files and caught a tampered binary; with dpkg on a production server no file differed, with 17 set-uid programs listed.Once a week. A root attacker who also rewrites the package database defeats it, as with debsums.
Process watchA baseline of outbound connections per program and port, containers' network namespaces included (a connection out of a container is named with its id). A shell or network tool talking out, or a miner or reverse-shell port, is always high. Programs from /tmp, from memory (memfd) or from deleted files are reported. Baselines of the kernel modules loaded, the containers running (from Docker's own files: a privileged one, or one on the host's network or mounting the Docker socket, is high) and WireGuard's peers: a new one after the learning period is reported.A snapshot every 60 s misses short-lived connections. eBPF tools are much stronger here.
Privilege and keysFailed su and sudo, each with the account's last SSH login and its place (su seen on the XMPP server; neither server has sudo). New-SSH-key alerts start after a learning period. PAM failures of the programs no other parser reads (the console, desktops, polkit, Cockpit, FTP), account changes (useradd, usermod, passwd, groups), auditd stopping, and authentication failures in the audit log.Wazuh's ruleset covers far more event types, and Windows and cloud logins. Account changes (a new account and group) have been reported on hosts; PAM failures and auditd alerts not yet.
MalwareTurns ClamAV's runs into alerts: infected files, scans killed for memory, stale signatures, missing runs. It ships a scan timer that runs at low priority and dies first when memory runs out. Files its watches see change can be scanned at once with ClamAV, YARA or both, outside vpsfort's memory limit, and are checked again just before the scanner reads them.No scanning engine of its own: ClamAV or YARA must be installed. The changed-file scans have run on hosts with ClamAV (a changed sshd_config, clean) and with ClamAV and YARA together (Debian's yara 4.5 with YARA Forge's core set compiled by yarac, a new file in /usr/local/bin: clean). An infected file's alert has not yet been seen on a host.
Tamper resistanceAlerts leave the host at once (encrypted bots, peers). The decision log can be mirrored to a peer at every sync ([cluster] mirror_to), and the peers hold each day's hash of it, so an edit shows and the lines survive a wiped node. A lost journal position and failed journalctl --verify checks are reported. Peers report a node that stops answering for 5 minutes, and a fact that fails its signature check is an alert (a node broken into, or a stolen key). vpsfort checks its own files daily: a key, config or state another user can read, a directory another user can write to, a binary that isn't root's. The daemon runs under a capability bound of nine.The raw journal stays on the host. A root attacker can stop vpsfort: the systemd watchdog restarts a hung daemon, not a deliberately stopped one. EDRs add tamper protection, and Wazuh ships every event off the host.

8. Exposure, patches and certificates

Bottom line: vpsfort explains, port by port, what the internet can reach and why, and catches certificate mistakes other monitors miss. It audits sshd, and alerts when its settings weaken. Use Wazuh or Lynis for CVE matching and whole-host hardening benchmarks, and unattended-upgrades to apply updates.

featurevpsfortWazuhLynisunattended-upgrades, needrestartUptime Kuma, blackbox_exporterexternal scan (nmap, Shodan)
each listener compared with the firewall rule that lets it in✓ ufw, firewalld zones, iptables, nftables rulesets, docker-proxy◐ port inventory◐ once per run––◐ outside view
Docker ports that bypass ufw✓––––◐ seen as open
alerts: new public listener, listener gone, dead allow rule, no firewall✓◐ listening-port change rule––◐ port down–
pending security updates, reboot needed, processes on replaced libraries✓ in the digest✓◐✓ installs and restarts––
CVE matching–✓◐––◐ service versions
SSH hardening audit, with alerts when settings weaken✓ sshd only✓ SCA◐ once per run––◐ algorithms only
hardening benchmark (CIS style), sysctls◐ the kernel's hardening sysctls, with alerts when one weakens; no benchmark✓ SCA✓–––
live certificate expiry, STARTTLS included✓–––✓◐
"renewed but not in use", DANE TLSA value at each renewal✓–––––
featurepros (vpsfort)cons (vpsfort), and who does better
ExposureReads the listeners from /proc and follows the firewall's own logic: rules in order, jumps, policies, rate limits. The TUI names the rule that lets each port in. It flags Docker-published ports, which ufw doesn't filter, and allow rules for ports nothing listens on: the kind of dead rules that were found by hand on the authors' hosts before. Under firewalld it reads the zones (services, ports, rich rules, source-bound zones), and nftables rulesets of your own as nft prints them (base chains in priority order, verdict maps). With peer_probe, the cluster's peers try the host's public ports from outside hourly: a port the firewall should block that a peer reaches is a high alert, one a provider's firewall in front blocks an info.A view from the host, plus its peers' (your own servers, not the internet at large); an external scan remains the ground truth.
PatchesA simulated apt-get, or dnf check-update from the cached metadata (no lock taken either way), in a unit of its own; a newer installed kernel than the running one; programs still running replaced libraries. All of it is in the daily digest.Reports only: it doesn't apply updates (use unattended-upgrades or dnf-automatic). No CVE database. apt and dnf only.
CertificatesLive endpoints every 6 h, XMPP STARTTLS included, plus the certificates on disk. It warns when a renewed certificate isn't served yet, explains ejabberd's ACME failures, gives the new DANE 3 1 1 value at each renewal, and compares the TLSA records published for each endpoint with the certificate served (a warning when none matches). This matters more since Let's Encrypt stopped sending expiry e-mails in June 2025. The host's own names are resolved hourly: one that no longer points here (a changed record, a hijack) is a high alert.Checked from the host itself, so routing problems elsewhere are not seen.
Hardeningsshd's settings as sshd -T applies them (Match blocks included), checked against the host's layout: a plain VPS, a hosting panel, or a key-only gateway user forced into a su/sftp wrapper. It names the file each setting comes from (cloud-init's override) and alerts when a setting weakens. The kernel's hardening sysctls the same way (forwarding, redirects, source routing, rp_filter, SYN cookies, ASLR, kptr_restrict, ptrace_scope, unprivileged BPF, protected links, ...), with the line to set.sshd and sysctls only: no other services, no CIS benchmark or score. Run Lynis or Wazuh SCA for the rest of the host.

9. Operations health

Bottom line: vpsfort covers the alerts a small server needs, and knows when its own view has gone dark. It is not a metrics system, but its web console hands what it sees to Prometheus.

featurevpsfortMonitNetdataPrometheus, AlertmanagerLogwatch, logcheck
a log source goes quiet, or stops naming addresses✓◐ file timestamps–◐ absent() on your own metrics–
OOM kills, with process and size✓–✓◐ a counter only◐ in the daily mail
disk space, memory pressure (PSI)✓✓✓✓–
failed systemd units that are not restarted✓✓✓✓–
restarts services itself–✓–––
daily digest✓ security and health–––✓
graphs and history◐ TUI trends; web console charts (3 hours to 7 days), what needed attention and the digests kept on disk (7 and 14 days by default, settable); Prometheus export with alerting rules shipped◐ M/Monit✓✓ with Grafana–
featurepros (vpsfort)cons (vpsfort), and who does better
SilenceTells "no attacks" from "not seeing logs", with thresholds measured per source on a real month. It also notices lines that arrive but name no address (ejabberd's hide_sensitive_log_data, a changed log format). An optional heartbeat URL (healthchecks.io, Uptime Kuma) lets a service outside notice when a node, or the whole cluster, goes dark.For its own sources only.
ResourcesOOM kills (they revealed weeks of a nightly ClamAV scan being killed), disk under 10 % and 5 %, memory availability and pressure stalls, failed units after a restart grace period, a clock that isn't synchronised, and its own state file and actions log when they can't be written.Basic: no per-process graphs; the console's own history (7 days of problems, 14 days of digests) is kept, but longer history needs Prometheus (vpsfort-web's /metrics, with deploy/prometheus-rules.yml to start from). Netdata and Prometheus are far richer.
DigestOne message a day per server, marked ⚠ when something needs attention: certificates, peers, feeds, dead rules, patches, top offenders.A fixed format, with no report builder.

10. Alerts and the people who get them

Bottom line: no other tool here encrypts alerts end to end, pseudonymizes them for an untrusted channel, or warns the user whose account is attacked. In exchange, vpsfort offers six channels where the others offer dozens.

featurevpsfortfail2banCrowdSecWazuhcommercial EDR, SIEM
admin channels◐ XMPP, Telegram, e-mail, Signal, Matrix, ntfy/Gotify/webhooks◐ e-mail, scripts✓ Slack, e-mail, webhooks, Splunk, Elastic, Sentinel, …✓ e-mail, Slack, PagerDuty, custom✓
end-to-end encryption✓ OMEMO, Signal, Olm/Megolm, OpenPGP––––
pseudonymized copies for an untrusted channel✓ Telegram––––
warns the attacked user, in their language✓ XMPP and Nextcloud accounts, 6 languages––––
budgets per topic, so a flood can't hide a critical alert✓–◐ grouping◐ e-mails per hour✓
featurepros (vpsfort)cons (vpsfort), and who does better
ChannelsXMPP through the admin bot, OMEMO-encrypted and kept out of the server's archive, or from any host through any XMPP server with the bot's client mode (OMEMO, else OpenPGP files). Telegram, with account names pseudonymized and servers named only by node id. E-mail, OpenPGP-encrypted as a whole, subject included. Signal (through signal-cli) while the admin's safety number is the pinned one, and Matrix, Olm/Megolm-encrypted to the devices the admin verified; both send OpenPGP files instead when they can't reach that identity. ntfy, Gotify or any webhook through the push bot, sanitized as for Telegram (files only encrypted). Any mix of the six. The XMPP bot survives sleep, Wi-Fi changes and server restarts without losing a message and without getting itself banned (back about two minutes after an ejabberd restart, seen on the XMPP server). Delivered for real: the XMPP bot (OMEMO), the Matrix bot (alerts, a digest and the weekly report as an encrypted attachment, through matrix.org to Element X) and the mail bot (alerts and the weekly report, read in Proton Mail on Android).No Slack, Teams or PagerDuty of their own (a webhook reaches them through a relay of yours). A new channel means a new bot, though the bots now share one library. The push bot is untried against a real ntfy or Gotify. The Signal bot needs signal-cli (Java on ARM) installed beside it. The Signal bot and the XMPP bot's client mode are untried against the real services.
PrivacyBuilt for operators who take GDPR seriously: nothing about users goes to a vendor, Telegram never sees account names, and reports travel as encrypted files.Encryption takes setup (OMEMO device trust, OpenPGP keys). If the XMPP bot doesn't take a message within 2 minutes, it goes out over plain XMPP, saying why.
UsersAccount holders learn when their account is attacked: password guessing, a possible compromise, and (opt-in) a login from a new network. No other tool here does this. XMPP accounts over XMPP, Nextcloud accounts through Nextcloud's own notifications (occ).SSH and the other services' users aren't messaged.
Noise controlOne message per incident and account every 6 h, hourly caps per topic for admins, hourly budgets per local user for host changes. Alerts relayed from peers are tagged with the node's name. A bot that stops sending is itself reported over the other channels.Fixed policies: routing is less flexible than Alertmanager's or CrowdSec's profiles.

11. Day-to-day operation

Bottom line: a capable terminal UI on each node, a read-only web console for the whole cluster with a REST API and Prometheus metrics, and careful upgrade tooling. Nothing changes from the web, by design: bans and settings stay on the servers, behind SSH. A browser, with its extensions and the exploits written for it, is no place to hold a firewall's controls, whatever the login method; a console broken into shows what its page shows and nothing more.

featurevpsfortfail2bansshguardCrowdSecWazuhCSF/LFD
live dashboard✓ terminal UI, web console––◐ cscli; web console (SaaS)✓ web✓ panel UI
web UI and fleet view✓ read only by design; dozens of nodes; a few equal admins (no roles) with a one-time code or a passkey, and proof of work, at the login––✓✓◐ per server
ban, unban, temporary allow✓ CLI and TUI, shared with peers✓–✓◐ API✓
pattern tester✓ test-pattern✓ fail2ban-regex–✓ cscli explain✓ wazuh-logtest–
REST API✓ read only by design––✓ local API✓–
metrics for Prometheus✓ through the web console, or a textfile for node_exporter◐ community exporters–✓◐ API–
community, Q&A, third-party guides◐ one detailed README✓◐✓✓◐ forks
featurepros (vpsfort)cons (vpsfort), and who does better
TUIWorks over SSH in an 80-column terminal. Up to 10 tabs: offenders with evidence, bans, decisions, accounts, exposure, countries with port histograms, a world map, alerts and patterns. Ban, unban and allow with a confirmation. It also reads saved decision logs of several servers offline.One node at a time (the web console shows them all); no roles, no multi-tenant view.
Toolingstatus (also as JSON), explain for one address, quiet to hold the admins' messages back during an upgrade, baseline export/import so a reinstalled host skips the learning days, replay, print-nft, notify-test for each channel, a digest preview. config-diff runs after every upgrade and lists new options, changed defaults and unknown keys. The installer dry-runs the nftables ruleset against the kernel first. vpsfort-sim runs a simulated cluster and the real web console on a laptop from a few days of decision logs, for trying the console without servers.No OpenTelemetry export, and no API that changes anything: the control socket is root-only, and the web console reads through a separate read-only socket.
Web consolevpsfort-web shows every node's bans, alerts, decisions, offenders, countries and networks, peers, certificates, disks and updates on one HTTPS page, with what needs attention first (as a queue, with 7 days of history of what appeared and went), activity charts over 3 hours to 7 days, a world map (bans at their coordinates with a city-level database, else at capitals; countries [geoblock] blocks hatched), a page per address, the nodes' settings side by side with the differences marked, each node's digests of the last two weeks, saved views and keyboard navigation; every table exports as CSV or JSON, and the whole cluster as one JSON file for a ticket. It only reads, by design: it holds a web key of its own, never the cluster key, and reaches vpsfort through a read-only socket, so a break-in at the console or in an admin's browser gives a view, never a ban, an unban or a setting. TLS 1.3; a password and a one-time code or a passkey (WebAuthn) per admin (one or several, all equal); a proof of work before each login that grows with failed attempts; sessions in memory that an admin can list and end; failed logins are locked out and banned by vpsfort itself. About 110 Prometheus metric families, with alerting rules shipped. Asked only while someone looks (plus each node's digest once a day).No roles: every admin sees everything. Its own history (a week of what needed attention, two weeks of digests, the marks) is kept on disk now; longer history needs Prometheus. A self-signed certificate unless you give it one. New, with two audits of its own so far; it runs on the authors' five servers, all but one through their agents. The CrowdSec Console and Wazuh's dashboard manage fleets and keep history on disk.
DocsOne README that explains every default, often with the measurement behind it.One long document: no website, no forum or chat. Some answers are only in the code.

12. Footprint, platforms and supply chain

Bottom line: the smallest and most self-contained option here after sshguard, written in a memory-safe language. It runs on fewer platforms, is packaged in fewer ways, and has the shortest track record.

featurevpsfortfail2bansshguardCrowdSecWazuhCSF/LFD
runtimestatic binary, ~10 MB (web console ~4 MB more)Python 3CGo: engine and bouncerC agent; manager; OpenSearch-based indexer; dashboardPerl
memory~10 MB for a real month, ~50 MB with geo; capped at 128 MBmoderatea few MBmoderatelight agent; the indexer needs ≥ 4 GB RAM (8 GB in the 5.0 beta)moderate
memory-safe language✓✓–✓– C agent✓
platformsLinux with systemd: x86_64, arm64, 32-bit ARMv7Linux, BSDLinux, BSD, macOSLinux, FreeBSD, Windows, containers, KubernetesLinux, Windows, macOS and moreLinux
ARM builds◐ arm64 and ARMv7 bundles, tested in CI only✓✓✓✓✓
distribution packages or apt repository✓ .deb, .rpm and Alpine .apk (OpenRC) from the releases, a signed apt and dnf repository on GitHub Pages (built by CI after each release), or a zip bundle with install.sh✓✓✓ vendor repositories✓ vendor repositories◐ forks
signed releases✓ build provenance (Sigstore), SHA256SUMS, a CycloneDX SBOM✓ distribution-signed✓ distribution-signed✓ signed repositories✓ signed repositories◐
licenseMITGPL-2.0BSD-styleMIT (engine)GPL-2.0GPL-3.0
in use sinceOctober 20262004the 2000s20202015about two decades, until 2025
featurepros (vpsfort)cons (vpsfort), and who does better
FootprintFits a 1 GB VPS next to the services it protects. The memory limit is backed by flood tests, per-address state is capped (max_tracked_ips), and the apt or dnf query runs in a unit of its own, outside the limit. Country blocking costs about 1 MB while its list is computed; the list itself sits in the kernel's sets.–
PlatformsThe static musl binary needs no matching libc: the same file runs on Debian 13, Ubuntu 22.04 and Fedora 44. Package checks with dpkg or rpm, patches with apt or dnf. Bundles for arm64 and 32-bit ARMv7 (hard-float), from the same CI.Linux with systemd only. The ARM bundles haven't run on an ARM server yet; RHEL and its rebuilds are untried.
Supply chainFew dependencies, cargo audit and cargo deny (permissive licenses, crates.io only) on every CI run, and a CycloneDX SBOM attached to every release next to the build provenance attestation. No TLS library in the binary: curl does the downloads. The parsers, the decision-log readers and their hourly index, the console's HTTP and agent protocols and abuse reports, and the bots' inputs are fuzzed (23 cargo-fuzz targets across the crates, each with a smoke run in every cargo test). Log text never reaches a shell: firewall changes are built from parsed addresses and fed to nft -f -. That class of bug is real elsewhere: fail2ban's CVE-2021-32749 (the mail-whois action), Wazuh's CVE-2026-54085 (active response scripts, fixed in 4.14.7). Nine internal security reviews in October and November 2026, the later ones covering the web console, the simulator, the bots and the packages too, each finding fixed with a test.No independent third-party audit yet, and no distribution maintainers reviewing its packages. A project this young has no CVE history to judge it by.

13. Commercial offerings: what money buys

offeringwhat it addspricing modelbetter than vpsfort atworse than vpsfort at
CrowdSec Console Premium, Platinum Blocklists, CTI APIcurated and industry blocklists, longer alert history, fleet console, supportper enrolled engine (Premium); Platinum Blocklists from about $1,900/month; IP reputation API from $49/month; support add-ons $1,000/month (vendor page, Oct 2026)network effect, fleet management, HTTP layerobservations shared with the vendor; cost grows with engines; no host-integrity checks
Wazuh Cloud and Wazuh supporthosted manager and indexer, retention, supportsubscription by number of agents and retentioncompliance dashboards (PCI DSS, GDPR, HIPAA, NIST 800-53), CVE detection, many operating systemsa heavy stack, data off-site; its active response is basic next to vpsfort's ban engine
Atomic Enterprise OSSEC (Atomicorp)OSSEC with a management console, compliance reports, curated rulesper node, by quotesupport, complianceproprietary and heavier
Imunify360 (CloudLinux), BitNinjaall-in-one suites for web-hosting servers: WAF, IDS/IPS, malware scanning, reputation networksper server per monthcPanel and Plesk web hosting, PHP malware cleanupbuilt for shared web hosting, not XMPP or VPN nodes; proprietary
EDR / XDR: CrowdStrike Falcon, SentinelOne, Microsoft Defender for Servers, Elastic Defendkernel or eBPF sensors, behaviour detection, threat hunting, optional 24/7 managed responseper endpoint per year, by quoteruntime visibility, tamper protection, malware, incident response, every common OScost for a few servers; telemetry goes to the vendor's cloud (GDPR processor terms); a vendor-updated kernel-level agent is a risk of its own (the July 2024 CrowdStrike outage); not built for log-driven IP banning
SIEM: Splunk, Datadog Cloud SIEM, Microsoft Sentinel, Elasticcentral search, correlation and retention across all systemsby data volume ingestedcorrelation across systems, long retention, investigationscost grows with log volume; they detect but don't prevent: something on the host still has to ban
Tripwire Enterprise (Fortra)compliance-grade FIM and configuration managementper node, by quoteaudit evidence, policy librariesprice and weight for a few VPS

What commercial products do better:

  • Support with an SLA, and someone to call during an incident.
  • Compliance evidence and certifications that auditors and insurers accept.
  • Threat research teams, and intelligence from millions of endpoints.
  • Every common OS, fleets of thousands, central consoles with roles.
  • Kernel-level visibility and tamper protection.

What they do worse, for a small self-hosted setup:

  • A recurring cost per host that can exceed the cost of the servers.
  • Security telemetry, including your users' addresses, leaves your servers.
  • Vendor lock-in, agent weight, and agents that need constant connectivity.
  • Most of them still don't ban an SSH or XMPP brute-forcer in the host firewall within seconds.
