What a mail looks like

Every mail is PGP/MIME (RFC 3156): multipart/encrypted, one OpenPGP message holding the whole content. Mail servers on the way, and the mailbox provider, see only:

  • the envelope and the outer headers: From, To, Date, Message-ID (random), the size, and the subject ... (OUTER_SUBJECT);
  • Auto-Submitted: auto-generated (no auto-replies), and, if the bot signs, an Autocrypt header with the bot's public key.

Inside, encrypted: the real subject ([vpn] vpsfort: password guessing blocked) and the headers again as "protected headers" (protected-headers="v1"), which Thunderbird, K-9 / Thunderbird for Android, FairEmail and Delta Chat show in place of ..., then the text, quoted-printable (any line length, any character). Text from attackers in alerts (user names they tried, URIs) never reaches a header unencoded and can't start a MIME part.

  • Alerts and the digest: one mail each, the message's text as the mail's text.
  • The weekly abuse report: the report as the mail's text (it is text). A file that isn't UTF-8 text would be attached instead.

The OpenPGP format is the one every client reads: v4 keys (RSA, or Ed25519/Curve25519), a v3 PKESK per recipient key, SEIPD v1 with MDC under AES-256, no compression (not AEAD / v6, which many clients can't read yet). tests/gpg_interop.rs has GnuPG decrypt the mails and check the signature, for RSA and Curve25519 keys.

Keys

  • OPENPGP_KEY (required): the recipients' public keys, in one file (armored or binary, one or more keys). Each address in MAIL_TO gets the key whose user id names it; with a single key, that key serves every address (logged as a warning when the user id differs). An address without a key stops the bot at the start (exit 78): nothing goes out in clear. A key that has expired, at the start or later: that recipient gets nothing (logged every 10 minutes) until the key is replaced; with no usable key at all the bot doesn't start, and when the last one expires while it runs, messages wait and vpsfort warns about them after bot_stuck_after. Only the key's own signatures count: certifications by others (gpg --sign-key, web of trust) are ignored, not taken for broken self-signatures.
  • SIGNING_KEY (optional): the bot's own key, made once with vpsfort-mail-bot keygen (Ed25519, no passphrase: it would sit next to the key anyway). Every mail is then signed, and the public key goes out in the Autocrypt header (autocrypt.org Level 1), so clients that read it (Thunderbird, K-9/FairEmail, Delta Chat) learn it from the first mail and show the signature as valid. Others: import signing.key.pub.asc. The key proves a mail comes from the bot; it can't read anything.

Only public keys of the recipients are on the server: whoever gets into it can't read mails already sent.

Sanitizing (optional)

The mails are encrypted end to end, so by default alerts and digests go out as they are, with XMPP account names and the servers' own addresses. SANITIZE=on replaces them exactly as vpsfort-tg-bot does for Telegram (the same code, src/redact.rs and src/servers.rs):

  • every XMPP address (alice@example.org, ejabberd:alice) becomes a pseudonym [acct:1a2b3c], an HMAC under PSEUDONYM_KEY (made on the first start, the bot's, 600); vpsfort-mail-bot pseudonym alice@example.org on the server tells which is which;
  • the servers' own addresses and host names in SERVER_NAMES become their node ids (nextcloud.example.org:443 → nextcloud.node1:443); attackers' addresses stay.

The weekly abuse report is never changed (as on Telegram). The Telegram bot sanitizes always.

The mail server

SMTP_TLSportlogin
the local Postfixnone (only allowed to 127.0.0.1, ::1, localhost)25usually SMTP_AUTH=none
a provider or another servertls (TLS from the start) or starttls (required: never falls back to plain)465 / 587SMTP_AUTH=auto (PLAIN or LOGIN, whichever is offered), plain, login

The server's certificate is checked against the system's CAs (/etc/ssl/certs) and SMTP_TLS_NAME (default: SMTP_HOST), or only against SMTP_CA_FILE (a server with a certificate of its own CA). A login is never sent over plain SMTP to another host: that setting is refused. The password goes in SMTP_PASSWORD_FILE (root:vpsfort-mail-bot 640).

What the server says decides what happens:

  • Out of reach, 4xx, TLS failing: the message waits; the bot tries again after 30 s, doubling up to 30 minutes. Alerts older than ALERT_MAX_HOURS (24) are dropped and counted in one mail; files after OUTBOX_MAX_DAYS (7).
  • 5xx for one mail (too large, rejected as spam): the bot sends a short note in its place saying what was refused and where to find it (vpsfort's actions log, or how to make the report again). If the note goes through, the refused mail is dropped.
  • 5xx for every mail (the sender or a recipient refused): the note fails too, the message waits, and the bot tries every 30 minutes.
  • A refused login (530, 534, 535, 538): no note is tried (it would be one more failed login towards the server's ban); the message waits, retried every 30 minutes. At the start it stops the bot with exit 78, which systemd doesn't restart.
  • No STARTTLS offered where SMTP_TLS=starttls: nothing is sent, not even the login, and the bot keeps retrying with backoff rather than giving up for good (someone in the way may be stripping it, and must not be able to stop the bot).

The bot opens one connection per mail (a few a day).

How vpsfort and the bot meet

The outbox (/opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/outbox, root:vpsfort-mail-bot 1770, sticky) has the format of the other bots: alert-*.msg with To: and Subject: lines, a blank line and the text, written under a dot name and renamed into place, owned by the bot's user. The bot claims one by renaming it to .<name>.sending, sends it, deletes it; if sending fails it renames it back (and on the next start it puts back what a stop left half-sent). The weekly report is any other regular file. vpsfort ([notify] mail_outbox, mail_user) puts a copy of every message for the first admin there (every admin message on a node with xmpp = false or no admins), and the abuse report job puts the report there. vpsfort warns the admins (over XMPP and to the Telegram bot, else as a system alert) when the bot leaves something unsent longer than bot_stuck_after.

Install from a release bundle

Instead of deploy.sh (below), without a Rust toolchain: every version tag vpsfort-mail-bot-v<version> gets its bundle, vpsfort-mail-bot-<version>-<commit>-<arch>.zip for each architecture (x86_64-linux-musl, aarch64-linux-musl for arm64, armv7-linux-musleabihf for 32-bit ARMv7 with hardware floating point), as an asset of the tag's GitHub Release (see "Releases" in the repository README); CI also keeps one for every commit as a workflow artifact (they expire and need a GitHub login), and scripts/bundle.sh builds it locally. It holds the static binary, the unit, config.env.example, SHA256SUMS and install.sh.

Download and verify (on your machine, then copy the zip over, or on the server): take the zip for the server's architecture (uname -m: x86_64, aarch64, or armv7l for armv7) from the GitHub Release of the version's tag, with the GitHub CLI or from the Releases page (each crate's releases are tagged vpsfort-mail-bot-v<version>):

arch=x86_64-linux-musl     # the server's: aarch64-linux-musl (arm64), armv7-linux-musleabihf (32-bit ARM)
gh release download vpsfort-mail-bot-v0.5.1 --repo makhort/vpsfort --pattern "*-$arch.zip*"   # the zip, its .sha256 and .sigstore.json
sha256sum -c vpsfort-mail-bot-*-$arch.zip.sha256              # the download is intact (macOS: shasum -a 256 -c)
gh attestation verify vpsfort-mail-bot-*-$arch.zip --repo makhort/vpsfort --source-ref refs/tags/vpsfort-mail-bot-v0.5.1

gh attestation verify (after gh auth login) checks the signed build provenance: that this repository's CI built exactly this zip from that tag. With --bundle vpsfort-mail-bot-*-$arch.zip.sigstore.json it checks the attestation attached to the Release instead of fetching it from GitHub. Only a public repository's releases have one (GitHub offers attestations for private repositories only with Enterprise Cloud).

On the server (apt install unzip or dnf install unzip if needed):

arch=x86_64-linux-musl     # as above: aarch64-linux-musl, armv7-linux-musleabihf on ARM
unzip vpsfort-mail-bot-*-$arch.zip && cd vpsfort-mail-bot-*-$arch
sudo ./install.sh          # install or upgrade; the first time also /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/config.env
sudo ./install.sh rollback # back to the previous version

The first install, as with deploy.sh, doesn't start the bot until config.env is filled in (then run it again). install.sh checks every file against SHA256SUMS in a copy only root can write, then runs the same installer as deploy.sh (./install.sh --check only checks the files).

Install from a package

The same release has a package per architecture, for the distribution's package manager instead of install.sh: vpsfort-mail-bot_<version>-1_<arch>.deb (amd64, arm64, armhf; Debian, Ubuntu and their derivatives) vpsfort-mail-bot-<version>-1.<arch>.rpm (x86_64, aarch64, armv7hl; Fedora, RHEL and its rebuilds) and vpsfort-mail-bot-<version>-r0.<arch>.apk (x86_64, aarch64, armv7; Alpine, with OpenRC: below), each with its .sha256 and .sigstore.json, downloaded and verified as the bundle above (--pattern "*.deb*", "*.rpm*" or "*.apk*"). CI keeps them for every commit as workflow artifacts, and scripts/package.sh builds them locally (cargo install cargo-deb cargo-generate-rpm --locked once, and python3 for the .apk, which scripts/apk.py at the repository's root writes; none needs dpkg, rpm or apk-tools, so it runs on macOS too; --target aarch64-unknown-linux-musl or armv7-unknown-linux-musleabihf for the ARM ones). Or from the signed apt and dnf repository on GitHub Pages, once it is set up (the repository README, "Deployment"), where apt upgrade and dnf upgrade then bring new versions.

sudo apt install ./vpsfort-mail-bot_0.5.1-1_amd64.deb     # Fedora, RHEL:  sudo dnf install ./vpsfort-mail-bot-0.5.1-1.x86_64.rpm
sudoedit /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/config.env                 # the first install put the template there
sudo systemctl enable --now vpsfort-mail-bot

The package puts the same files in the same places as install.sh (/opt/vpsfort-mail-bot with the binary and config.env.example, the user vpsfort-mail-bot, /var/log/vpsfort-mail-bot, the logrotate rule), except the unit, which is in /usr/lib/systemd/system. Its post-install step does what install.sh does apart from copying files: the user, config.env from the template the first time, the owners and modes, the outbox and the log directory; and it prints what is left to do ("Setup": the addresses, the mail server, the recipients' OpenPGP keys). dpkg-reconfigure vpsfort-mail-bot (rpm: dnf reinstall ./vpsfort-mail-bot-*.rpm) runs that step again, e.g. after copying a key there. apt remove vpsfort-mail-bot (dnf remove) stops the bot and keeps /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot; apt purge vpsfort-mail-bot removes it with the state and keys (rpm: by hand).

Nothing starts on its own. The first install puts the template in place and prints what to fill in; you enable the service once it is filled in. An upgrade keeps the config and restarts the service only if it was running and the config is filled in. Over a bundle or deploy.sh install the package takes over: the config, state and keys stay; the bundle's unit in /etc/systemd/system (it would shadow the package's) and its rollback files go, and the service is enabled again on the package's unit if it was (vpsfort's integrity watch reports the removed unit as a warning once). Replacing the config: when a terminal is there, the install asks whether to replace the config with the new template (the old one kept with the suffix .old); Enter, a timeout of a minute, no terminal or DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive keep it. VPSFORT_PACKAGE_CONFIG=replace (or keep) answers without asking: sudo VPSFORT_PACKAGE_CONFIG=replace apt install ./<file>.deb. Rollback is installing the previous version's package (apt install ./<old>.deb --allow-downgrades, dnf downgrade ./<old>.rpm): there is no .prev file.

Alpine (apk, OpenRC): apk add --allow-untrusted ./vpsfort-mail-bot-<version>-r0.x86_64.apk puts the same files in the same places, with the OpenRC script /etc/init.d/vpsfort-mail-bot instead of the unit: the settings exported from config.env as EnvironmentFile= does, supervised by supervise-daemon and started again 5 seconds after a crash; systemd's sandboxing has no equivalent there. The package is unsigned, hence --allow-untrusted: sha256sum -c and the attestation verify it. Its install scripts are POSIX sh and do what the post-install step above does: the user, config.env from the template the first time, the owners and modes, the log directory; an upgrade restarts a running bot. Then rc-update add vpsfort-mail-bot default && rc-service vpsfort-mail-bot start. apk del vpsfort-mail-bot stops it and keeps /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot. Not yet run on an Alpine host: CI installs the package in an Alpine container.

Setup

On your Mac:

./deploy/deploy.sh vpn

It runs scripts/ci.sh, builds the static binary, copies it with the unit and install.sh to the server and prints the command to paste into a root shell there. The first install creates the user vpsfort-mail-bot, /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/config.env from config.env.example, and doesn't start the bot until it's filled in. Then, as root:

  1. Fill in config.env: MAIL_FROM, MAIL_TO, the mail server.
  2. Copy the recipients' public keys to /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/recipients.asc (Thunderbird: Account settings → End-to-end encryption → OpenPGP Key Manager → File → Export; or gpg --armor --export you@example.org), and the SMTP password to smtp-password.
  3. Optionally, the signing key: set -a; . /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/config.env; set +a; /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/vpsfort-mail-bot keygen --out /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/signing.key and SIGNING_KEY=/opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/signing.key in config.env.
  4. bash /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/install.sh again (it sets owners and modes of the keys and files), then:
set -a; . /opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/config.env; set +a
/opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/vpsfort-mail-bot check        # connects, logs in, sends nothing
/opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/vpsfort-mail-bot send-test    # one test mail
systemctl enable --now vpsfort-mail-bot
  1. In [notify] of /etc/vpsfort/vpsfort.toml: mail_outbox = "/opt/vpsfort-mail-bot/outbox" and mail_user = "vpsfort-mail-bot", restart vpsfort, and vpsfort -c /etc/vpsfort/vpsfort.toml notify-test --mail.

For SPF/DKIM, MAIL_FROM should be an address the mail server may send as; otherwise the mails may land in spam. install.sh rollback goes back to the previous binary and unit.

Settings

Every option is also an environment variable (config.env is the unit's EnvironmentFile); vpsfort-mail-bot --help lists them with their defaults. deploy/config.env.example explains each one.

Retention and what the bot keeps

  • Outbox files until they are sent (alerts 24 h at most, files 7 days).
  • Its log, /var/log/vpsfort-mail-bot/bot.log (owner only): what was sent to whom, and the mail server's answers; rotated weekly, no line older than 14 days. No message text.
  • Nothing else: no state file. With SANITIZE=on, the pseudonym key. The service makes it on its first start; the one-off commands (check, send-test, pseudonym), run as root, never do, so it stays the bot's.

The unit is hardened like vpsfort-tg-bot's: no capabilities, a read-only system but its two directories, IPv4 and IPv6 sockets only, no writable-executable memory, and 128 MB of memory at most (MemoryMax).

Build and test

./scripts/ci.sh

Format, clippy, the tests, cargo audit, and the static x86_64-unknown-linux-musl binary (TARGET=aarch64-unknown-linux-musl ./scripts/ci.sh, or armv7-unknown-linux-musleabihf, for ARM). tests/fake_smtp.rs runs the bot against a fake mail server on 127.0.0.1 (plain, STARTTLS and TLS with a test CA, PLAIN and LOGIN, a server that strips STARTTLS, refusals) and runs the binary as systemd would; it needs to bind 127.0.0.1. tests/gpg_interop.rs needs gpg (skips without it). SMTP is lettre (blocking, rustls with ring: no OpenSSL), OpenPGP is rpgp, both pure Rust.

Fuzzing. What comes from outside (vpsfort's outbox files with attackers' log text in them, other files there, the recipients' key file, the signing key) goes through four cargo-fuzz targets (code in src/fuzzing.rs). Besides no panics, each checks the promises with rules of its own:

  • alert_mail: an outbox file to the finished mail, with SANITIZE off and on. The mail is all ASCII with CRLF line ends and lines under 998 characters; it has exactly the bot's own outer headers (subject ...) and, between the boundaries, only the version part and the armored message. Decrypted, it has the fixed protected headers, the subject on one line, and the text back unchanged; with SANITIZE=on, no account address and no server address or host name of the fuzzed setup is left.
  • file_mail: a file (name, NUL, content): text as the mail's text, anything else attached under a safe name, back byte for byte.
  • openpgp_keys: the key file; whatever is taken must encrypt to every usable address.
  • signing_key: whatever is taken signs mails that verify, in a well-formed Autocrypt header.

tests/fuzz_smoke.rs runs them on stable on realistic inputs, the seeds in fuzz/seeds/ and a few thousand mutations, so cargo test keeps them working; a failing input is saved as target/fuzz-smoke-<target>.bin (VPSFORT_MAIL_BOT_WRITE_SEEDS=1 cargo test --test fuzz_smoke regenerates the seeds; the keys in them, the signing key's secret part too, are throwaway). The real search needs nightly:

rustup toolchain install nightly --profile minimal && cargo install cargo-fuzz --locked   # once
cargo +nightly fuzz run alert_mail fuzz/corpus/alert_mail fuzz/seeds/alert_mail -- -dict=fuzz/alert_mail.dict -max_len=8192 -max_total_time=600

New inputs collect in fuzz/corpus/, crashes in fuzz/artifacts/ (both git-ignored). The regression-* seeds are inputs the first runs (2026-10-17) failed on, all fixed: a subject holding =?utf-8?b?…?= went into the header as it was, so mail clients would have shown it decoded, as other text (now encoded); a damaged signing key whose secret part no longer matched its public part was taken, and every mail would have carried a bad signature (now refused at the start); with SANITIZE=on, a server address replaced by its node id completed an address (admin@::ffff:192.0.2.10 → admin@node1; fixed in vpsfort-tg-bot too). One was the oracle's: a pseudonym cut by the 200-character subject limit.

Tested on a server (since 2026-10-20): a Debian VPS deployed with deploy.sh, the unit running. check, send-test, vpsfort's notify-test --mail (picked up from the outbox and sent within seconds), a digest (digest --send), real alerts (a source gone quiet, new listeners, a cluster node not responding) and the weekly abuse report, all through a provider (SMTP_TLS=tls, port 465, LOGIN) to a SimpleLogin alias forwarding to Proton Mail. Proton Mail on Android decrypts and shows them; GnuPG on a Mac decrypts the exported mails and shows the bot's signature as good. Proton re-armors the encrypted part and exports it quoted-printable with CRLF line ends, which turns every = in the armor (the base64 padding and the checksum line) into =3D and makes gpg report a CRC error or a malformed CRC. Undo that before decrypting a mail exported from Proton (only there: base64 itself never holds =3D, apart from a checksum that happens to start with 3D):

sed 's/=3D/=/g' mail.eml | gpg --decrypt

vpsfort-signal-bot

A Signal bot that brings vpsfort's admin messages to the admin's Signal account: the alerts, the daily digest and the weekly abuse report. Signal encrypts every message end to end; the bot adds a check that the other end is still the admin (the safety number), and when it isn't, sends only files encrypted to the admin's OpenPGP key, or nothing. It does for Signal what vpsfort-tg-bot does for Telegram on vpsfort's side, through the same kind of outbox:

  • A client only. It uses an account of its own on Signal's servers through signal-cli, which it runs as its child process. No server runs here, nothing listens.
  • Send only. It reads nothing people send it: no commands. The servers are managed over SSH.
  • No link to ejabberd. It runs as its own user vpsfort-signal-bot.

vpsfort feeds it only when [notify] signal_outbox is set. Without it, nothing changes. It can run next to the other bots, on any node; on the relay node (vpsfort's deploy/vpsfort-tg.toml) the XMPP server never connects to Signal.

Why signal-cli

Signal's protocol libraries (libsignal, and presage on top of it) are AGPL-3.0, and Signal's service (registration, sealed sender, its key servers) can't reasonably be reimplemented from specifications, as vpsfort-xmpp-bot's OMEMO was. So the bot, which stays MIT, talks to signal-cli (GPL-3.0, a separate program) over its JSON-RPC interface (signal-cli jsonRpc on stdin and stdout, src/rpc.rs), and signal-cli does the Signal protocol. The bot holds no Signal keys itself; signal-cli keeps them in its data directory (SIGNAL_CLI_DATA, the bot's user only, mode 700).

What it sends

From vpsfortIn Signal
alerts, the daily digesta message, the subject (first line) in bold
the weekly abuse reportan attachment (abuse-report-<date>.txt)
either, while the safety number isn't the pinned onean attachment message-<time>.txt.pgp / report-<time>.txt.pgp encrypted to OPENPGP_KEY, with a neutral caption, and once a day a short message saying why

With SANITIZE=yes alerts and digests also lose XMPP account names (pseudonyms) and the servers' addresses (node ids), as on Telegram; off by default, since they are end-to-end encrypted.

The safety number

The first time the bot writes to the admin, it sends a message without content (vpsfort-signal-bot: connected...): that makes the Signal session, so signal-cli knows the admin's identity key before anything real goes out. The bot then pins the safety number (the number the Signal app shows under the chat's "View safety number") in state.json, or takes it from ADMIN_SAFETY_NUMBER, and logs it: compare it with your app.

Before each message it asks signal-cli for the admin's current identity:

  • the pinned safety number: the message goes as a Signal message. If signal-cli had marked the identity untrusted (after a change you then confirmed with ADMIN_SAFETY_NUMBER), the bot tells signal-cli it is verified first.
  • another one (you reinstalled Signal or changed phones, or someone took over your number): nothing readable goes to the new identity. The bot tells signal-cli to accept the new key for transport only, and sends each message and file as an attachment encrypted to OPENPGP_KEY, with one notice a day naming both safety numbers. Without OPENPGP_KEY, messages wait (alerts for ALERT_MAX_HOURS, files for OUTBOX_MAX_DAYS). It stays so until you check the new safety number in the app, set ADMIN_SAFETY_NUMBER to it in config.env and restart the bot.

The OpenPGP files are v4 / SEIPD v1 with AES-256 (what OpenKeychain on Android and iPGMail on iPhone read), with a neutral name and caption.

How vpsfort and the bot meet

They share one directory, /opt/vpsfort-signal-bot/outbox (root:vpsfort-signal-bot 1770, sticky), in vpsfort-xmpp-bot's file format (src/outbox.rs, vpsfort's src/botbox.rs): vpsfort writes a copy of each message to the first of [notify] admins (every admin message when admins is empty or xmpp = false) as alert-<time>-<n>.msg; the bot looks every 5 seconds, claims one by renaming it to .<name>.sending, sends it and deletes it, or renames it back to try again (30 s later; 5 minutes after Signal's rate limit). The abuse report job puts the weekly report there too when [notify] signal_outbox (or ABUSE_REPORT_SIGNAL_OUTBOX) is set; the bot looks every minute. There is no fallback on vpsfort's side: vpsfort warns the admins its other ways when copies wait longer than bot_stuck_after.

Check it as root: vpsfort -c /etc/vpsfort/vpsfort.toml notify-test --signal.

signal-cli

Install a current signal-cli on the node (Signal stops accepting old clients after a few months: keep it updated). From its releases:

  • x86_64: the native build, signal-cli-<version>-Linux-native.tar.gz: one binary, no Java. Put it at /usr/local/bin/signal-cli.
  • ARM (aarch64, armv7): the Java build, signal-cli-<version>.tar.gz, needs Java 21 or later and libsignal's native library for the architecture (signal-cli's wiki, "Provide native lib for libsignal"); point SIGNAL_CLI at its bin/signal-cli.

The unit allows writable-executable memory for the Java build's JIT (unlike the other bots' units) and 1 GB of memory (MemoryMax; the other bots get 128 MB); otherwise it is hardened the same way.

Install from a release bundle

As for the other crates (see vpsfort-tg-bot's README, "Install from a release bundle"): the tag is vpsfort-signal-bot-v<version>, the zip vpsfort-signal-bot-<version>-<commit>-<arch>.zip; unzip it on the server and run sudo ./install.sh. The first install doesn't start the bot: continue with step 3 below.

Install from a package

The same release has a package per architecture, for the distribution's package manager instead of install.sh: vpsfort-signal-bot_<version>-1_<arch>.deb (amd64, arm64, armhf; Debian, Ubuntu and their derivatives) vpsfort-signal-bot-<version>-1.<arch>.rpm (x86_64, aarch64, armv7hl; Fedora, RHEL and its rebuilds) and vpsfort-signal-bot-<version>-r0.<arch>.apk (x86_64, aarch64, armv7; Alpine, with OpenRC: below), each with its .sha256 and .sigstore.json, downloaded and verified as the bundle above (--pattern "*.deb*", "*.rpm*" or "*.apk*"). CI keeps them for every commit as workflow artifacts, and scripts/package.sh builds them locally (cargo install cargo-deb cargo-generate-rpm --locked once, and python3 for the .apk, which scripts/apk.py at the repository's root writes; none needs dpkg, rpm or apk-tools, so it runs on macOS too; --target aarch64-unknown-linux-musl or armv7-unknown-linux-musleabihf for the ARM ones). Or from the signed apt and dnf repository on GitHub Pages, once it is set up (the repository README, "Deployment"), where apt upgrade and dnf upgrade then bring new versions.

sudo apt install ./vpsfort-signal-bot_0.3.1-1_amd64.deb     # Fedora, RHEL:  sudo dnf install ./vpsfort-signal-bot-0.3.1-1.x86_64.rpm
sudoedit /opt/vpsfort-signal-bot/config.env                 # the first install put the template there
sudo systemctl enable --now vpsfort-signal-bot

The package puts the same files in the same places as install.sh (/opt/vpsfort-signal-bot with the binary and config.env.example, the user vpsfort-signal-bot, /var/log/vpsfort-signal-bot, the logrotate rule), except the unit, which is in /usr/lib/systemd/system. Its post-install step does what install.sh does apart from copying files: the user, config.env from the template the first time, the owners and modes, the outbox and the log directory; and it prints what is left to do ("Setup": the numbers, your OpenPGP key, then signal-cli's registration as the bot's user (the install prints the commands; signal-cli itself is installed separately)). dpkg-reconfigure vpsfort-signal-bot (rpm: dnf reinstall ./vpsfort-signal-bot-*.rpm) runs that step again, e.g. after copying a key there. apt remove vpsfort-signal-bot (dnf remove) stops the bot and keeps /opt/vpsfort-signal-bot; apt purge vpsfort-signal-bot removes it with the state and keys (rpm: by hand).

Nothing starts on its own. The first install puts the template in place and prints what to fill in; you enable the service once it is filled in. An upgrade keeps the config and restarts the service only if it was running and the config is filled in. Over a bundle or deploy.sh install the package takes over: the config, state and keys stay; the bundle's unit in /etc/systemd/system (it would shadow the package's) and its rollback files go, and the service is enabled again on the package's unit if it was (vpsfort's integrity watch reports the removed unit as a warning once). Replacing the config: when a terminal is there, the install asks whether to replace the config with the new template (the old one kept with the suffix .old); Enter, a timeout of a minute, no terminal or DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive keep it. VPSFORT_PACKAGE_CONFIG=replace (or keep) answers without asking: sudo VPSFORT_PACKAGE_CONFIG=replace apt install ./<file>.deb. Rollback is installing the previous version's package (apt install ./<old>.deb --allow-downgrades, dnf downgrade ./<old>.rpm): there is no .prev file.

Alpine (apk, OpenRC): apk add --allow-untrusted ./vpsfort-signal-bot-<version>-r0.x86_64.apk puts the same files in the same places, with the OpenRC script /etc/init.d/vpsfort-signal-bot instead of the unit: the settings exported from config.env as EnvironmentFile= does, supervised by supervise-daemon and started again 5 seconds after a crash; systemd's sandboxing has no equivalent there. The package is unsigned, hence --allow-untrusted: sha256sum -c and the attestation verify it. Its install scripts are POSIX sh and do what the post-install step above does: the user, config.env from the template the first time, the owners and modes, the log directory; an upgrade restarts a running bot. Then rc-update add vpsfort-signal-bot default && rc-service vpsfort-signal-bot start. apk del vpsfort-signal-bot stops it and keeps /opt/vpsfort-signal-bot. Not yet run on an Alpine host: CI installs the package in an Alpine container.

Setup

  1. A phone number for the bot that can receive one SMS or call (not your own Signal number), or a Signal account of its own on a phone to link signal-cli to. Install signal-cli (above).
  2. Deploy from the Mac (runs scripts/ci.sh first, builds the static binary): 
    ./deploy/deploy.sh          # to the relay node "tg"; name another host as the argument
    The first install creates the user, /opt/vpsfort-signal-bot (with signal-cli's data directory) and config.env.
  3. Fill in config.env as root (SIGNAL_ACCOUNT, ADMIN, your OpenPGP public key as OPENPGP_KEY, root:vpsfort-signal-bot 640) and run bash install.sh again. It prints the commands for the next step with the settings filled in. Register the bot's number, as the bot's user (the signal-cli subcommand runs signal-cli with the bot's data directory and account): 
    runuser -u vpsfort-signal-bot -- env SIGNAL_ACCOUNT=+49... /opt/vpsfort-signal-bot/vpsfort-signal-bot signal-cli register --captcha 'signalcaptcha://...'
runuser -u vpsfort-signal-bot -- env SIGNAL_ACCOUNT=+49... /opt/vpsfort-signal-bot/vpsfort-signal-bot signal-cli verify 123456
    The captcha token comes from https://signalcaptchas.org/registration/generate.html (signal-cli's wiki, "Registration with captcha"). Or link signal-cli as a device of the bot's own Signal account: ... signal-cli link -n vpsfort prints a sgnl:// link to open as a QR code (qrencode -t ansiutf8 '<link>') in that account's app, under Linked devices. Optionally ... signal-cli updateProfile --given-name vpsfort. Then systemctl enable --now vpsfort-signal-bot (or bash install.sh again).
  4. Check the safety number: the first message pins it (pinned the safety number in /var/log/vpsfort-signal-bot/bot.log); compare it with the one your app shows for the bot's chat, or set ADMIN_SAFETY_NUMBER before the first message.
  5. Tell vpsfort, in [notify] of /etc/vpsfort/vpsfort.toml (deploy a vpsfort that knows the key first): 
    signal_outbox = "/opt/vpsfort-signal-bot/outbox"
signal_user = "vpsfort-signal-bot"
    then systemctl restart vpsfort and vpsfort ... notify-test --signal. Adding vpsfort-signal-bot.service to vpsfort's [sources] services gets an alert when it fails.

install.sh rollback goes back to the previous binary and unit. Signal sees the host's address; the host needs outgoing HTTPS to Signal's servers.

Settings

All in config.env (deploy/config.env.example has each with a comment; --help lists the matching flags):

VariableDefault
SIGNAL_CLI/usr/local/bin/signal-clithe program
SIGNAL_CLI_DATA/opt/vpsfort-signal-bot/signal-cliits data directory (700)
SIGNAL_CLI_ARGS--ignore-attachments --ignore-storiesmore arguments for jsonRpc
SIGNAL_ACCOUNTthe bot's number
ADMINyour number or ACI: the only one the bot writes to
ADMIN_SAFETY_NUMBER(pinned on first sight)the safety number with you
OPENPGP_KEYyour OpenPGP public key, for the fallback; without it messages wait
SANITIZEnoyes: pseudonyms and node ids in alerts (XMPP_DOMAINS, SERVER_NAMES, PSEUDONYM_KEY)
ALERT_MAX_HOURS24older alerts are dropped unsent
OUTBOX_MAX_DAYS7older files are deleted unsent
BOT_STATE_PATH/opt/vpsfort-signal-bot/state.json
OUTBOX_DIRoutbox next to the state
LOG_DIR/var/log/vpsfort-signal-botbot.log, 14 days (signal-cli's own lines included)

Exit status 78 (a setting is wrong, or signal-cli didn't start: the account isn't registered, an option it doesn't know) is not restarted by the unit; the log has signal-cli's last lines. When signal-cli stops later, the bot stops with exit 1 and systemd starts both again.

Retention and what the bot keeps

  • On Signal: messages stay in the chat until deleted (Signal's disappearing messages, set in your app for the chat, apply to the bot's messages too).
  • On the server: bot.log (14 days), state.json (the pinned safety number), signal-cli's data directory (the account's keys and sessions, its contacts; signal-cli keeps no message history, and receives with attachments ignored), with SANITIZE the pseudonym key, and outbox files until they are sent or expire.
PathOwnerMode
/opt/vpsfort-signal-bot/root:vpsfort-signal-bot1770 (sticky)

Build and test

./scripts/ci.sh            # fmt, clippy, tests, cargo audit, static Linux binary

The static build needs rustup target add x86_64-unknown-linux-musl (or the ARM targets) and rustup component add llvm-tools, as the other crates. tests/fake_signal_cli.rs runs the bot against a fake signal-cli on a pipe that keeps identities and trust as signal-cli does: the first message without content, the safety number pinned, an alert with a bold subject, the report as an attachment, a changed identity (nothing sent without an OpenPGP key; with one, signal-cli told to accept the key, a notice, and files the admin's key opens, nothing readable in any request), the new safety number confirmed with ADMIN_SAFETY_NUMBER (signal-cli verifies it, messages again), Signal's rate limit, messages to the bot ignored, and signal-cli going away. src/cli.rs's tests start a program that exits at once and check its words reach the error.

Not yet tried with a real signal-cli or Signal account. The JSON-RPC calls follow signal-cli's documentation (man signal-cli-jsonrpc); send's textStyle and data: attachments need signal-cli 0.12 or later.

Fuzzing. fuzz/ has cargo-fuzz targets (code in src/fuzzing.rs): cli_line (signal-cli's output lines as replies to send and listIdentities: a safety number is always 60 digits) and alert_file (outbox files). tests/fuzz_smoke.rs runs them on stable over realistic inputs, fuzz/seeds/ and thousands of mutations (VPSFORT_SIGNAL_BOT_WRITE_SEEDS=1 regenerates the seeds).

cargo +nightly fuzz run cli_line fuzz/corpus/cli_line fuzz/seeds/cli_line -- -max_total_time=600

vpsfort-matrix-bot

A Matrix bot that brings vpsfort's admin messages to the admin's Matrix account: the alerts, the daily digest and the weekly abuse report, end-to-end encrypted (Olm and Megolm, as Element, FluffyChat and other clients do it). When end-to-end encryption to the admin isn't possible, a message goes out as a file encrypted to the admin's OpenPGP key instead, or waits. It does for Matrix what vpsfort-tg-bot does for Telegram on vpsfort's side, through the same kind of outbox, and nothing else:

  • A client only. It logs in to an existing homeserver (matrix.org or your own) with an account of its own. No homeserver runs here, nothing listens.
  • Send only. It doesn't read the room (no /sync): no commands, and nothing anyone writes there reaches it. The servers are managed over SSH.
  • No link to ejabberd. It runs as its own user vpsfort-matrix-bot, which can't read ejabberd's files.

vpsfort feeds it only when [notify] matrix_outbox is set. Without it, nothing changes. It can run next to the other bots, on any node: on the relay node (vpsfort's deploy/vpsfort-tg.toml), the XMPP server never connects to the homeserver.

Contents

  2. End-to-end encryption, and who gets the keys
  3. When end-to-end encryption isn't possible
  4. How vpsfort and the bot meet
  5. Install from a release bundle
  6. Install from a package
  7. Setup
  8. Settings
  9. Retention and what the bot keeps
  10. Why not matrix-sdk

What it sends

From vpsfortIn the room
alerts, the daily digesta text message, Megolm-encrypted
the weekly abuse reportan encrypted attachment (AES-256-CTR, its key inside the Megolm-encrypted event)
either, when end-to-end encryption isn't possiblea file message-<time>.txt.pgp / report-<time>.txt.pgp encrypted to OPENPGP_KEY, with a neutral caption, and once a day a short notice saying why

The homeserver sees that the bot sent something to the room, when, and how large: never what. With SANITIZE=yes alerts and digests also lose XMPP account names (they become pseudonyms) and the servers' addresses (node ids), as on Telegram; off by default, since they are end-to-end encrypted (vpsfort-matrix-bot pseudonym <jid> tells which account a pseudonym is).

End-to-end encryption, and who gets the keys

The bot is a Matrix device of its own (vpsfort-matrix-bot login makes it). It makes a direct chat with the admin (ADMIN), encrypted from its first event, and invites them. For each message it:

  1. asks the homeserver for the admin's devices and cross-signing keys, and checks every signature itself (src/crypto.rs): a device must sign its own keys; with cross-signing, the admin's self-signing key must carry their master key's signature and the device the self-signing key's. The homeserver can withhold keys, not forge them.
  2. decides which devices are the admin's:
    • with cross-signing (the usual case: every current client sets it up): only the devices the admin verified, under the admin's master key, which the bot pins the first time it sees it (or takes from ADMIN_MASTER_KEY). A changed master key, as after a reset of your cross-signing or a homeserver handing out keys of its own, gets nothing: messages come as OpenPGP files until you set ADMIN_MASTER_KEY to the new key (the bot logs it, and so does its notice) and restart the bot.
    • without cross-signing: every device that signs its own keys, each pinned by device id on first sight (a device id that comes back with other keys gets nothing). The bot logs a warning: anyone who controls the homeserver could add a device then.
  3. shares the room's Megolm session key with those devices over Olm (a one-time key claimed for each, checked against the device's signature), and sends the message as a Megolm event. A new session is made after 100 messages, after a week, and whenever a device that had the key is no longer trusted (removed, or unverified), so it can't read what follows.

The bot's own device is signed by the bot account's cross-signing keys (made at the first login), so clients show its messages as from a device its owner verified rather than with a red warning. You can also verify the bot user in your client; the bot doesn't need it.

The Olm and Megolm code is vodozemac (matrix.org's pure-Rust implementation, Apache-2.0, audited); the rest (key checks, sharing, the client API calls) is in this crate. state.json keeps the device's keys, the sessions and the pins (mode 0600); a Megolm ratchet is saved before each message leaves, so no message index is ever used twice.

When end-to-end encryption isn't possible

  • E2EE=no;
  • the admin's master key changed or vanished (above);
  • with cross-signing, none of the admin's devices is verified;
  • no device could take the room key (no one-time key to start an Olm session with);
  • the room isn't encrypted (it always is, unless someone with power in it changed that).

Then a message or file goes out as one OpenPGP message to OPENPGP_KEY (v4, RSA or Curve25519, SEIPD v1 with AES-256: what OpenKeychain on Android and iPGMail on iPhone read), uploaded as a file in a plain event, with a notice at most once a day saying why. Without OPENPGP_KEY messages wait in the outbox (alerts for ALERT_MAX_HOURS, files for OUTBOX_MAX_DAYS), and the notice says that instead. Nothing goes to the homeserver in clear but these notices, which name keys, never content. vpsfort warns the admins over its other ways when copies wait longer than bot_stuck_after.

How vpsfort and the bot meet

They share one directory, /opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot/outbox (root:vpsfort-matrix-bot 1770, sticky), in vpsfort-xmpp-bot's file format (src/outbox.rs, vpsfort's src/botbox.rs):

  1. vpsfort writes a copy of each message to the first of [notify] admins (every admin message when admins is empty or xmpp = false) as alert-<time>-<n>.msg, gives the file to matrix_user, and renames it into place.
  2. The bot looks every 5 seconds, claims a message by renaming it to .<name>.sending, sends it and deletes it. If sending fails it renames it back and tries again (after the homeserver's retry_after_ms, or 30 s). A half-sent one is put back at the next start.
  3. There is no plain-text fallback on vpsfort's side: copies wait until the bot sends them.
  4. vpsfort-abuse-report.sh puts the weekly report into this outbox too when [notify] matrix_outbox (or ABUSE_REPORT_MATRIX_OUTBOX) is set. The bot looks every minute.

Check it as root: vpsfort -c /etc/vpsfort/vpsfort.toml notify-test --matrix puts a test in the outbox and waits until the bot has sent it.

Install from a release bundle

As for the other crates (see vpsfort-tg-bot's README, "Install from a release bundle"): the tag is vpsfort-matrix-bot-v<version>, the zip vpsfort-matrix-bot-<version>-<commit>-<arch>.zip; unzip it on the server and run sudo ./install.sh. The first install doesn't start the bot: continue with step 3 below.

Install from a package

The same release has a package per architecture, for the distribution's package manager instead of install.sh: vpsfort-matrix-bot_<version>-1_<arch>.deb (amd64, arm64, armhf; Debian, Ubuntu and their derivatives) vpsfort-matrix-bot-<version>-1.<arch>.rpm (x86_64, aarch64, armv7hl; Fedora, RHEL and its rebuilds) and vpsfort-matrix-bot-<version>-r0.<arch>.apk (x86_64, aarch64, armv7; Alpine, with OpenRC: below), each with its .sha256 and .sigstore.json, downloaded and verified as the bundle above (--pattern "*.deb*", "*.rpm*" or "*.apk*"). CI keeps them for every commit as workflow artifacts, and scripts/package.sh builds them locally (cargo install cargo-deb cargo-generate-rpm --locked once, and python3 for the .apk, which scripts/apk.py at the repository's root writes; none needs dpkg, rpm or apk-tools, so it runs on macOS too; --target aarch64-unknown-linux-musl or armv7-unknown-linux-musleabihf for the ARM ones). Or from the signed apt and dnf repository on GitHub Pages, once it is set up (the repository README, "Deployment"), where apt upgrade and dnf upgrade then bring new versions.

sudo apt install ./vpsfort-matrix-bot_0.3.1-1_amd64.deb     # Fedora, RHEL:  sudo dnf install ./vpsfort-matrix-bot-0.3.1-1.x86_64.rpm
sudoedit /opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot/config.env                 # the first install put the template there
sudo systemctl enable --now vpsfort-matrix-bot

The package puts the same files in the same places as install.sh (/opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot with the binary and config.env.example, the user vpsfort-matrix-bot, /var/log/vpsfort-matrix-bot, the logrotate rule), except the unit, which is in /usr/lib/systemd/system. Its post-install step does what install.sh does apart from copying files: the user, config.env from the template the first time, the owners and modes, the outbox and the log directory; and it prints what is left to do ("Setup": the homeserver and accounts, your OpenPGP key, then login as the bot's user (the install prints the command)). dpkg-reconfigure vpsfort-matrix-bot (rpm: dnf reinstall ./vpsfort-matrix-bot-*.rpm) runs that step again, e.g. after copying a key there. apt remove vpsfort-matrix-bot (dnf remove) stops the bot and keeps /opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot; apt purge vpsfort-matrix-bot removes it with the state and keys (rpm: by hand).

Nothing starts on its own. The first install puts the template in place and prints what to fill in; you enable the service once it is filled in. An upgrade keeps the config and restarts the service only if it was running and the config is filled in. Over a bundle or deploy.sh install the package takes over: the config, state and keys stay; the bundle's unit in /etc/systemd/system (it would shadow the package's) and its rollback files go, and the service is enabled again on the package's unit if it was (vpsfort's integrity watch reports the removed unit as a warning once). Replacing the config: when a terminal is there, the install asks whether to replace the config with the new template (the old one kept with the suffix .old); Enter, a timeout of a minute, no terminal or DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive keep it. VPSFORT_PACKAGE_CONFIG=replace (or keep) answers without asking: sudo VPSFORT_PACKAGE_CONFIG=replace apt install ./<file>.deb. Rollback is installing the previous version's package (apt install ./<old>.deb --allow-downgrades, dnf downgrade ./<old>.rpm): there is no .prev file.

Alpine (apk, OpenRC): apk add --allow-untrusted ./vpsfort-matrix-bot-<version>-r0.x86_64.apk puts the same files in the same places, with the OpenRC script /etc/init.d/vpsfort-matrix-bot instead of the unit: the settings exported from config.env as EnvironmentFile= does, supervised by supervise-daemon and started again 5 seconds after a crash; systemd's sandboxing has no equivalent there. The package is unsigned, hence --allow-untrusted: sha256sum -c and the attestation verify it. Its install scripts are POSIX sh and do what the post-install step above does: the user, config.env from the template the first time, the owners and modes, the log directory; an upgrade restarts a running bot. Then rc-update add vpsfort-matrix-bot default && rc-service vpsfort-matrix-bot start. apk del vpsfort-matrix-bot stops it and keeps /opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot. Not yet run on an Alpine host: CI installs the package in an Alpine container.

Setup

  1. An account for the bot on a homeserver, made in any client (not your own account). Note its user id (@vpsfort:example.org) and password. Your own client should have cross-signing set up (current Element, Element X, FluffyChat do it by default) and your devices verified.
  2. Deploy from the Mac (runs scripts/ci.sh first, builds the static binary): 
    ./deploy/deploy.sh          # to the relay node "tg"; name another host as the argument
    The first install creates the user, /opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot and config.env.
  3. Fill in config.env as root: MATRIX_HOMESERVER, MATRIX_USER, ADMIN, and your OpenPGP public key as OPENPGP_KEY (/opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot/admin.asc, root:vpsfort-matrix-bot 640), then log the bot in, as its own user (it asks for the password, which it doesn't keep): 
    runuser -u vpsfort-matrix-bot -- sh -c 'set -a; . /opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot/config.env; exec /opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot/vpsfort-matrix-bot login'
    It prints the bot's device and keys (vpsfort-matrix-bot info again later). If the account already has cross-signing keys from another client, login --reset-cross-signing replaces them; a homeserver behind Matrix Authentication Service then asks you to approve the reset in the account's web page (the bot prints the link). Run bash install.sh again (or systemctl enable --now vpsfort-matrix-bot).
  4. Accept the invitation in your client. The first message pins your master key; check it in the bot's log (pinned ... master cross-signing key) against your client's, or set ADMIN_MASTER_KEY before the first message.
  5. Tell vpsfort, in [notify] of /etc/vpsfort/vpsfort.toml (deploy a vpsfort that knows the key first): 
    matrix_outbox = "/opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot/outbox"
matrix_user = "vpsfort-matrix-bot"
    then systemctl restart vpsfort and vpsfort ... notify-test --matrix. Adding vpsfort-matrix-bot.service to vpsfort's [sources] services gets an alert when it fails.

install.sh rollback goes back to the previous binary and unit. The host needs outgoing HTTPS to the homeserver; the homeserver sees the host's address.

Settings

All in config.env (deploy/config.env.example has each with a comment; --help lists the matching flags):

VariableDefault
MATRIX_HOMESERVERthe client URL, https (http only to localhost)
MATRIX_USERthe bot's account, for login
ADMINyour user id: the only one the bot writes to
ADMIN_MASTER_KEY(pinned on first sight)your master cross-signing key
E2EEyesno: everything as OpenPGP files
OPENPGP_KEYyour OpenPGP public key, for the fallback; without it messages wait
SANITIZEnoyes: pseudonyms and node ids in alerts (XMPP_DOMAINS, SERVER_NAMES, PSEUDONYM_KEY)
ALERT_MAX_HOURS24older alerts are dropped unsent
OUTBOX_MAX_DAYS7older files are deleted unsent
BOT_STATE_PATH/opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot/state.json
OUTBOX_DIRoutbox next to the state
LOG_DIR/var/log/vpsfort-matrix-botbot.log, 14 days

Exit status 78 (a setting is wrong, no login yet, or the homeserver no longer knows the login: a device deleted in a client, a password change that logged out every device) is not restarted by the unit: run login again, then start it.

Retention and what the bot keeps

  • On the homeserver: the room's events (encrypted) and uploads (encrypted) stay as long as the homeserver keeps them; delete the room in your client to drop them for you.
  • On the server: bot.log (14 days), state.json (login token, device keys, Olm and Megolm sessions, the bot's self-signing key, your pinned keys: no message text), with SANITIZE the pseudonym key, and outbox files until they are sent or expire. Whoever reads state.json (root) could read what the bot sends while the current Megolm session lasts.
PathOwnerMode
/opt/vpsfort-matrix-bot/root:vpsfort-matrix-bot1770 (sticky)
config.env, OPENPGP_KEYroot:vpsfort-matrix-bot640
state.json, pseudonym.keyvpsfort-matrix-bot600
outbox/root:vpsfort-matrix-bot1770; files vpsfort-matrix-bot 600
/var/log/vpsfort-matrix-bot/, bot.logvpsfort-matrix-bot700, 600

The unit drops every capability, makes the file system read-only except its two directories, allows only IPv4 and IPv6 sockets, denies writable-executable memory, and is held to 128 MB of memory (MemoryMax).

Why not matrix-sdk

matrix-rust-sdk's crypto needs a persistent store, and its only native one is SQLite (C code), which would end the static musl build from macOS without a C cross toolchain; it would also bring a sync loop and room state the bot doesn't need. The bot uses the same audited Olm and Megolm (vodozemac) and does the few steps of a send-only device itself (src/api.rs, crypto.rs, bot.rs, login.rs: under 2000 lines with their tests).

Build and test

./scripts/ci.sh            # fmt, clippy, tests, cargo audit, static Linux binary

The static build needs rustup target add x86_64-unknown-linux-musl (or the ARM targets) and rustup component add llvm-tools, as the other crates. tests/fake_homeserver.rs runs the bot against a fake homeserver on 127.0.0.1 whose admin devices are vodozemac accounts of their own: login (device keys that verify, cross-signing keys, the device signed by them, the password step when the homeserver asks), the room made encrypted with the admin invited, an alert and the report that decrypt on the verified phone and not on the unverified laptop (nothing of them in any request), a newly verified device getting the current key, a removed one forcing a new session, rate limiting, a changed master key giving OpenPGP files that the admin's key opens (and ADMIN_MASTER_KEY bringing end-to-end back), messages that wait without any route, E2EE=no, an admin without cross-signing, a device id with new keys left out, a second login keeping the room and pins, a wrong password leaving the working device alone, and a revoked token stopping the bot.

On matrix.org (since 2026-10-29): notify-test --matrix, real alerts and a daily digest arrived in Element X end-to-end encrypted, without warnings, and so did the weekly abuse report (51 KB, covering two nodes) as an encrypted attachment, which Element X decrypted and opened in its file viewer. Its event shows the file encrypted with AES-256-CTR and the key inside the Megolm-encrypted event, as above; Element X lists the bot's identity as unverified (the admin never verified the bot account) without warning about it.

Not yet seen for real: the OpenPGP files (a changed master key, no verified device), and a homeserver other than matrix.org.

Fuzzing. fuzz/ has cargo-fuzz targets (code in src/fuzzing.rs): keys_query (the homeserver's key replies: only self-signed devices come out, cross-signed ones only under a master key), alert_file (outbox files), attachment (encrypt and decrypt, damage caught). Under cargo-fuzz vodozemac accepts every signature (its fuzzing cfg), so the targets reach past the checks; the signature checks themselves are in src/crypto.rs's tests. tests/fuzz_smoke.rs runs the targets on stable over realistic inputs, fuzz/seeds/ and thousands of mutations (VPSFORT_MATRIX_BOT_WRITE_SEEDS=1 regenerates the seeds).

cargo +nightly fuzz run keys_query fuzz/corpus/keys_query fuzz/seeds/keys_query -- -max_total_time=600

vpsfort-push-bot

A send-only bot that brings vpsfort's admin messages (the alerts, the daily digest and the weekly abuse report) to a push service you run or trust, or to any webhook:

  • ntfy (https://ntfy.sh, or a server of your own): a message per alert, the report as an attachment;
  • Gotify (a server of your own): a message per alert; it takes no attachments, so files stay on the server and the admin is told once a day;
  • a webhook: a JSON POST per alert and per file, with a Bearer token.

None of these encrypts end to end: the service's server reads what the bot sends. So, as on Telegram (vpsfort-tg-bot, whose code this bot shares), account names become pseudonyms and the servers appear only by their node ids, and files go out only OpenPGP-encrypted to the admin's key, or not at all. It does for push services what vpsfort-tg-bot does for Telegram on vpsfort's side, through the same kind of outbox, and nothing else: it reads nothing back, takes no orders for the servers, and runs as its own user vpsfort-push-bot.

vpsfort feeds it only when [notify] push_outbox is set. Without it, nothing changes. It can run next to the other bots, on any node; on a relay node (vpsfort's deploy/vpsfort-tg.toml), the XMPP server never connects to the service.
